  • Safety: text in {{ }} is always escaped, so a product named <script> is shown, not run. The directive v-html inserts raw HTML; never use it with data that users wrote (Chapter 14).

4.3 Props, slots and emits

Components become useful when they can be reused with different data. Props are the inputs of a component, declared with defineProps and a TypeScript type. First, the shop’s data:

products.ts
export type Product = {
  id: number;
  name: string;
  price: number;
  category: "books" | "gifts";
  isNew?: boolean;
  inStock: boolean;
};

export const products: Product[] = [
  { id: 1, name: "Code from Zero", price: 25, category: "books", isNew: true, inStock: true },
  { id: 2, name: "Bit mug", price: 8, category: "gifts", inStock: true },
  { id: 3, name: "Bit sticker", price: 3, category: "gifts", inStock: false },
  { id: 4, name: "Bit cap", price: 12, category: "gifts", isNew: true, inStock: true },
];

A card for one product. It receives the product as a prop, and tells its parent when the button is pressed by emitting an event, declared with defineEmits:

ProductCard.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import type { Product } from "./products";

defineProps<{ product: Product }>();
const emit = defineEmits<{ add: [id: number] }>();
</script>

<template>
  <article class="card">
    <span v-if="product.isNew" class="badge">New</span>
    <h3>{{ product.name }}</h3>
    <p class="price">{{ product.price.toFixed(2) }} EGP</p>
    <button type="button" :disabled="!product.inStock" @click="emit('add', product.id)">
      {{ product.inStock ? "Add to cart" : "Sold out" }}
    </button>
  </article>
</template>

A section with a title, and a <slot>: a hole that the parent fills with any content it likes:

ShopSection.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
defineProps<{ title: string }>();
</script>

<template>
  <section>
    <h2>{{ title }}</h2>
    <slot />
  </section>
</template>

And the shop, which puts them together:

Shop.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import ProductCard from "./ProductCard.vue";
import ShopSection from "./ShopSection.vue";
import { products } from "./products";
</script>

<template>
  <ShopSection title="Bit's shop">
    <div class="grid">
      <ProductCard v-for="product in products" :key="product.id" :product="product" />
    </div>
  </ShopSection>
</template>

Three rules make components work well together:

  • Data flows down, events flow up. A parent passes props to a child; the child never changes them. When the child needs something to change, it emits an event, and the parent, which owns the data, decides. The parent listens with @add="…", exactly as it would listen to @click.
  • A colon means JavaScript. title="Bit's shop" passes a string; :product="product" passes the value of the expression product. Forgetting the colon passes the word itself.
  • Slots compose. ShopSection does not know or care what is inside it. The same section can hold a grid of products, a form, or a message.

Tip: small components with good names

When a template grows past what fits on one screen, or when you want to give a part of it a name, it is time for a new component. A file called ProductCard.vue explains itself; a <div> forty lines deep does not.

4.4 Lists and keys

v-for="product in products" repeats an element for every item of an array. Each repeated element needs a :key, a value that is unique among its siblings and stays the same for the same item, usually its id.

Keys tell Vue which item is which between two updates. Without them, when you delete the first product, Vue sees “one item fewer” and updates the remaining elements in place, so anything those elements remember, such as the text typed in an input or a running animation, ends up next to the wrong product. With keys, Vue knows that product 1 is gone, and removes exactly its element.

Watch out: the index is not a key

v-for="(p, i) in products" :key="i" stops the warning but not the problem: after a deletion, the item that was second becomes first and takes key 0. Use an id from the data. An index is only safe for a list that never changes order or length.

4.5 Choosing what to show

v-if, v-else-if and v-else choose between elements. A component that shows a list of orders must handle four situations, and good interfaces design all four:

orders.ts
export type Orders =
  | { status: "loading" }
  | { status: "error"; message: string }
  | { status: "success"; orders: string[] };
OrderList.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import type { Orders } from "./orders";

defineProps<{ state: Orders }>();
</script>

<template>
  <p v-if="state.status === 'loading'" class="muted"><span class="spinner" />Loading your orders…</p>
  <p v-else-if="state.status === 'error'" class="error">
    Could not load your orders: {{ state.message }}
    <button type="button" class="secondary">Try again</button>
  </p>
  <p v-else-if="state.orders.length === 0" class="muted">You have no orders yet.</p>
  <ul v-else>
    <li v-for="order in state.orders" :key="order">{{ order }}</li>
  </ul>
</template>

Because state is a discriminated union (Chapter 2), TypeScript follows the v-if chain in the template: inside the error branch, state.message is known to exist, and inside the last branch, state.orders does.

Tip: v-if or v-show?

v-if adds and removes the element. v-show keeps it in the page and only toggles display: none. Use v-show for something that switches very often, such as a dropdown, and v-if for everything else, so hidden parts cost nothing.

Quick check

  1. What is the difference between title="product" and :title="product"?
  2. Why is the index a bad key for a list where items can be deleted?
  3. How does a child component tell its parent that a button was pressed?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

4.6 Events and a first piece of state

@click (short for v-on:click) runs code when an event happens. To make a component remember something between updates, such as whether it is liked, give it state with ref:

LikeButton.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { ref } from "vue";

const props = defineProps<{ initial: number }>();
const likes = ref(props.initial);
const liked = ref(false);

function toggle() {
  liked.value = !liked.value;
  likes.value += liked.value ? 1 : -1;
}
</script>

<template>
  <button type="button" :class="{ secondary: !liked }" :aria-pressed="liked" @click="toggle">
    {{ liked ? "♥ Liked" : "♡ Like" }} · {{ likes }} likes
  </button>
</template>
Likes.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import LikeButton from "./LikeButton.vue";
</script>

<template>
  <p style="display: flex; gap: 8px">
    <LikeButton :initial="41" />
    <LikeButton :initial="7" />
  </p>
</template>

ref(41) returns a small box, a reactive reference, whose current value is in .value. In the script you write likes.value; in the template Vue unwraps it for you, so you write likes. Changing .value is all it takes: Vue knows which parts of the template read likes, and updates them. The next chapter explains how this works.

Every instance of a component gets its own state. The two buttons above share the code, but not the liked and likes boxes.

4.7 Computed values, and pure templates

When a value can be calculated from other values, calculate it: the followers shown are the followers plus one if you follow; the visible products are the products that match the filter. Vue offers two ways, and the difference matters:

CartTotal.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { computed } from "vue";
import { counts } from "./counts";

const props = defineProps<{ prices: number[] }>();

// Runs again every time the template is drawn, for every place it is used
function totalMethod() {
  counts.method++;
  return props.prices.reduce((sum, p) => sum + p, 0);
}

// Runs once, and again only when the cart changes
const total = computed(() => {
  counts.computed++;
  return props.prices.reduce((sum, p) => sum + p, 0);
});
</script>

<template>
  <p>{{ totalMethod() }} = {{ totalMethod() }} = {{ totalMethod() }}</p>
  <p>{{ total }} = {{ total }} = {{ total }}</p>
</template>
computed.ts
import { createSSRApp, h } from "vue";
import { renderToString } from "vue/server-renderer";
import CartTotal from "./CartTotal.vue";
import { counts } from "./counts";

console.log(await renderToString(createSSRApp({ render: () => h(CartTotal, { prices: [25, 8, 12] }) })));
console.log("Calls to the method:", counts.method);
console.log("Calls to the computed value:", counts.computed);
Terminal
<!--[--><p>45 = 45 = 45</p><p>45 = 45 = 45</p><!--]-->
Calls to the method: 3
Calls to the computed value: 1

A function called in the template runs every time the template is drawn, once for every place it appears. A computed value runs once, remembers its result, and runs again only when something it read changes. Use computed for anything derived from state.

The counters in this example are there to show the difference; in real code, a template and a computed value must have no side effects. They should only calculate. Vue decides when and how often to draw, and code that changes things while drawing gives different results each time. Changes belong in event handlers.

Professional habit: keep the logic in the script

A template that reads {{ items.filter((i) => i.inStock).length }} works, but it hides logic where it is hard to test and to name. Give it a name in the script, const inStock = computed(…), and the template stays a simple description of the screen.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
:price="25" forgotten as price="25"The prop receives the string "25", not a numberUse the colon for anything that is not a fixed string
@click="add()" confused with :onclickCode that runs at the wrong time@click="add" or @click="add(product.id)"
A list without keys, or with index keysA warning, and items mixed up after changes:key="item.id"
likes++ in the script, where likes is a refA type error, or a ref replaced by a numberlikes.value++ in the script; likes in the template
Changing a prop in the childA warning, and a value the parent overwritesEmit an event; let the parent change it
v-if and v-for on the same elementConfusing priority and wasted workFilter in a computed, or put v-if on a wrapping <template>

Summary

  • In Vue, the screen follows the data: you describe it in a template, and Vue keeps the page matching it.
  • A single-file component has a <script setup>, a <template> and optional <style scoped>.
  • Templates use {{ }} for text, : for bindings, @ for events, and :class and :style objects. Text is escaped.
  • Props pass data down, typed with defineProps; slots pass content; emits pass events up.
  • v-for renders lists, always with a stable key. v-if chooses what to show; design all four states.
  • ref gives a component state; computed derives values and remembers them. Templates only calculate.

Quick check answers: 1. The first passes the word “product” as a string; the second passes the value of the JavaScript expression product. 2. After a deletion, the following items move to new indexes, so Vue matches the wrong elements and may keep the wrong state on screen. 3. It emits an event, declared with defineEmits, and the parent listens with @name.

Practice projects

Build each project in a Vite project created with the vue-ts template: npm create vite@latest my-vue -- --template vue-ts. Before writing code, draw the screen on paper and circle the components. Then build from the smallest component upwards.

Project 4.1 · Easy

A profile card

Build a profile card with a slot for the biography, and a follow button that changes the number of followers.

You will practise: typed props, a default slot, exporting a type from a component, ref and computed.

  1. Write a type Person with a name, role, city, number of projects and number of followers. Export it from ProfileCard.vue, so pages can import it with the component.
  2. Show the first letter of the name in a circle, marked aria-hidden="true": the name is already written next to it.
  3. Put a <slot /> under the role and city, so each page can put its own text inside the card.
  4. Add const following = ref(false), and a computed number of followers that adds one while following. Do not change the prop.
  5. Give the button :aria-pressed="following", so screen readers announce whether it is on.

Hint: in the template, @click="following = !following" is enough: Vue unwraps the ref when you assign to it there too. In the script you would write following.value = !following.value.

ProfileCard.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { computed, ref } from "vue";

export type Person = { name: string; role: string; city: string; projects: number; followers: number };

const props = defineProps<{ person: Person }>();
const following = ref(false);
const followers = computed(() => props.person.followers + (following.value ? 1 : 0));
</script>

<template>
  <article class="profile">
    <div class="cover" />
    <div class="body">
      <div class="avatar" aria-hidden="true">{{ person.name[0] }}</div>
      <h2>{{ person.name }}</h2>
      <p class="meta">{{ person.role }} · {{ person.city }}</p>
      <slot />
      <div class="stats">
        <span><b>{{ person.projects }}</b>projects</span>
        <span><b>{{ followers }}</b>followers</span>
      </div>
      <button type="button" :class="{ secondary: following }" :aria-pressed="following" @click="following = !following">
        {{ following ? "Following" : "Follow" }}
      </button>
    </div>
  </article>
</template>

Project 4.2 · Medium

A catalogue with filters

Show a grid of products with filter buttons for each category and a line that says how many products are visible.

You will practise: lists and keys, a ref typed with a union, a computed filtered list, and buttons with aria-pressed.

  1. Reuse products.ts. Create the type Filter = "all" | Product["category"]: it updates itself if a category is added.
  2. Keep the filter buttons as data, an array of { value, label }, and render them with v-for.
  3. Store only the chosen filter in a ref. Make visible a computed.
  4. Show “Showing 3 of 4 products” using visible.length and products.length.
  5. Group the buttons with role="group" and a label, and mark the active one with :aria-pressed.

Hint: a common mistake is to keep visible in a ref too, and then forget to update it when the products change. If a value can be calculated from other state, compute it.

Project 4.3 · Challenge

Accessible tabs

Build a reusable tabs component that works with the mouse, the keyboard and screen readers, in both left-to-right and right-to-left pages.

You will practise: named and dynamic slots, ARIA roles, useId for unique ids, template refs to move the focus, and keyboard events.

  1. Make TabList take labels: string[] and a label for the group. The content of each tab comes from a named slot: #tab-0, #tab-1…
  2. Render a role="tablist" with one role="tab" button per tab, and one role="tabpanel" per tab, hidden unless active.
  3. Link each tab and panel with aria-controls and aria-labelledby. Make the ids with useId(), so two tab lists on one page never clash.
  4. Only the active tab is in the Tab order (:tabindex="0"); the others get -1. The arrow keys move between tabs, and Home and End jump to the ends.
  5. Collect the buttons with ref="tab" inside the v-for and useTemplateRef("tab"), which gives an array, and call focus() on the new tab. In a right-to-left page, the right arrow moves to the previous tab.

Hint: this is the keyboard pattern described in the WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices, the reference for accessible widgets. When you build any widget, look it up there first: the keyboard behaviour users expect is already decided.

TabList.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { ref, useId, useTemplateRef } from "vue";

const props = defineProps<{ labels: string[]; label: string }>();
const active = ref(0);
const id = useId();
const buttons = useTemplateRef<HTMLButtonElement[]>("tab");

function select(index: number) {
  const next = (index + props.labels.length) % props.labels.length;
  active.value = next;
  buttons.value?.[next]?.focus();
}

function onKeydown(event: KeyboardEvent) {
  const rtl = document.dir === "rtl";
  if (event.key === "ArrowRight") select(active.value + (rtl ? -1 : 1));
  if (event.key === "ArrowLeft") select(active.value + (rtl ? 1 : -1));
  if (event.key === "Home") select(0);
  if (event.key === "End") select(props.labels.length - 1);
}
</script>

<template>
  <div class="tabs">
    <div role="tablist" :aria-label="label" @keydown="onKeydown">
      <button
        v-for="(text, i) in labels"
        :id="`${id}-tab-${i}`"
        ref="tab"
        :key="text"
        type="button"
        role="tab"
        :aria-selected="i === active"
        :aria-controls="`${id}-panel-${i}`"
        :tabindex="i === active ? 0 : -1"
        @click="select(i)"
      >
        {{ text }}
      </button>
    </div>
    <div
      v-for="(text, i) in labels"
      :id="`${id}-panel-${i}`"
      :key="text"
      role="tabpanel"
      :aria-labelledby="`${id}-tab-${i}`"
      :hidden="i !== active"
    >
      <slot :name="`tab-${i}`" />
    </div>
  </div>
</template>

Exercises

  1. Easy Write a Price.vue component with the props value and currency that shows the value with two decimals, and use it in ProductCard.
  2. Easy Give ProductCard a "-20%" badge for products with a discount property, without showing anything for the others.
  3. Medium In Shop.vue, keep a cart count in a ref, increase it when a card emits add, and show “3 items in your cart” above the grid.
  4. Medium Add a fourth status to Orders, { status: "offline" }, and handle it in OrderList.vue. Where does TypeScript help you, and where does it not?
  5. Challenge Give ShopSection a second, named slot, #actions, shown next to the title, and use it in Shop.vue for a “See all” link. Make the slot optional: when it is not given, nothing extra is drawn.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 5

You have used ref and computed. In the next chapter you will see how Vue’s reactivity really works, and learn the whole Composition API: reactive objects, watchers, the life of a component, and two-way binding with v-model.

5

Chapter 5

Reactivity and the Composition API

In this chapter you will learn to

  • explain how Vue knows what to update: tracking what code reads, and triggering what depends on it
  • choose between ref and reactive, and avoid the ways reactivity is lost
  • keep state minimal and compute everything else
  • react to changes with watchers, and clean up after them
  • use the life of a component, and two-way binding with v-model on your own components

In the last chapter you changed likes.value and the button updated, as if by magic. It is not magic, and knowing how it works is the difference between a developer who uses Vue and one who can explain why a screen did not update. This chapter opens the box: how Vue tracks data, the few rules that follow from it, and the tools of the Composition API, the functions such as ref, computed and watch that you call inside <script setup>.

Bit

Most bugs about “Vue did not update” come from two or three rules. Learn the rules in section 5.2 once, and those bugs disappear from your life.

5.1 Track and trigger

Vue’s reactivity works in two steps. When code reads a reactive value, Vue notes who read it: this is tracking. When the value is changed, Vue reruns everything that read it: this is triggering. A component’s template is such code: it is tracked while it renders, and rerun when anything it read changes. You can see the mechanism without any component:

reactivity.ts
import { computed, effect, reactive } from "vue";

const cart = reactive({
  lines: [] as { name: string; price: number; quantity: number }[],
  coupon: "",
});
const total = computed(() => cart.lines.reduce((sum, l) => sum + l.price * l.quantity, 0));

// Runs now, and again whenever something it read changes
effect(() => console.log(`Total: ${total.value}`));

cart.lines.push({ name: "Bit mug", price: 8, quantity: 1 });
cart.lines.push({ name: "Code from Zero", price: 25, quantity: 1 });
cart.lines[0]!.quantity = 3;
cart.coupon = "BIT10"; // Nothing printed: the total never read the coupon
Terminal
Total: 0
Total: 8
Total: 33
Total: 49

Four lines printed, for three changes plus the first run. The last change printed nothing: total never read coupon, so changing the coupon cannot change the total, and Vue does not waste time recomputing it. This is why Vue updates so precisely: every piece of the screen depends on exactly the data it read.

How does Vue see a read? reactive() wraps an object in a JavaScript Proxy, which can intercept every property read and write. ref() does the same with a small object whose only property is value. That is why refs need .value in the script: it is the property Vue watches.

5.2 ref, reactive, and losing reactivity

ref(value)reactive(object)
HoldsAny value: a number, a string, an object, an arrayObjects and arrays only
Read and writecount.value in the script, count in the templatecart.lines everywhere
Can be replacedYes: list.value = []No: assigning a new object to the variable loses the link

The official recommendation, which this book follows, is simple: use ref for all state. It works for every kind of value, it can be replaced, and the .value reminds you that the value is reactive. The second rule is about a trap that looks innocent:

lost.ts
import { reactive, toRefs } from "vue";

const product = reactive({ name: "Bit mug", stock: 3 });

// Destructuring copies the number once; toRefs keeps a link to the object
const { stock } = product;
const { stock: stockRef } = toRefs(product);

product.stock = 2;
console.log("Copied value:", stock);
console.log("Linked ref:", stockRef.value);
Terminal
Copied value: 3
Linked ref: 2

Destructuring reads product.stock once and copies the number into a plain variable, which knows nothing about later changes. The same happens when you pass product.stock to a function that keeps it. When you need a separate variable that stays linked, toRefs turns each property into a ref connected to the object.

Updates are batched

Changing reactive data does not update the screen immediately. Vue collects all the changes made in the same moment and applies them together, once:

batching.ts
import { nextTick, ref, watch } from "vue";

const count = ref(0);
// Three changes, one run of the watcher, with the first and last values
watch(count, (now, before) => console.log(`watch: ${before} → ${now}`));

count.value++;
count.value++;
count.value++;
console.log("Right after the changes:", count.value);
await nextTick();
console.log("After nextTick");
Terminal
Right after the changes: 3
watch: 0 → 3
After nextTick

Three changes, one run of the watcher, with the first and last values. The page works the same way: after you change data, the DOM is updated a moment later, all at once. If code must see the updated page, for example to measure an element or to move the focus into a field that just appeared, await nextTick() first.

5.3 Minimal state, computed values

The most important design skill for state is to keep as little of it as possible. Everything that can be calculated from other state should be a computed, never a second copy that must be kept in step by hand:

cart-model.ts
export type Product = { id: number; name: string; price: number };
export type Line = { product: Product; quantity: number };

export const catalogue: Product[] = [
  { id: 1, name: "Code from Zero", price: 25 },
  { id: 2, name: "Bit mug", price: 8 },
  { id: 3, name: "Bit cap", price: 12 },
];

export const FREE_DELIVERY = 50;
Cart.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { computed, ref } from "vue";
import { catalogue, FREE_DELIVERY, type Line, type Product } from "./cart-model";

// Only the lines are state; everything else is computed from them
const lines = ref<Line[]>([]);

const count = computed(() => lines.value.reduce((n, l) => n + l.quantity, 0));
const total = computed(() => lines.value.reduce((sum, l) => sum + l.product.price * l.quantity, 0));
const missing = computed(() => Math.max(0, FREE_DELIVERY - total.value));

function add(product: Product) {
  const line = lines.value.find((l) => l.product.id === product.id);
  if (line) line.quantity++;
  else lines.value.push({ product, quantity: 1 });
}

function remove(id: number) {
  lines.value = lines.value.filter((l) => l.product.id !== id);
}
</script>

<template>
  <section class="cart">
    <div class="row">
      <button v-for="p in catalogue" :key="p.id" type="button" class="secondary" @click="add(p)">
        Add {{ p.name }}
      </button>
    </div>
    <p v-if="lines.length === 0" class="muted">Your cart is empty.</p>
    <ul v-else>
      <li v-for="line in lines" :key="line.product.id">
        <span>{{ line.product.name }} × {{ line.quantity }}</span>
        <button type="button" class="secondary" @click="remove(line.product.id)">Remove</button>
      </li>
    </ul>
    <p>Items: {{ count }} · <span class="total">Total: {{ total }} EGP</span></p>
    <div class="bar" aria-hidden="true"><span :style="{ width: `${Math.min(100, (total / 50) * 100)}%` }" /></div>
    <p v-if="missing === 0" class="free">Free delivery!</p>
    <p v-else class="muted">{{ missing }} EGP more for free delivery</p>
  </section>
</template>

The only state is lines. If the count were stored separately, every function that changes the lines would also have to remember to update the count, and one day one would forget. Computed values cannot be forgotten: they are always calculated from the truth.

Notice also line.quantity++ in add. Unlike some frameworks, Vue lets you change objects and arrays in place: the proxy sees the write, and everything that read quantity updates. Replacing a whole array, as remove does with filter, works too, because lines is a ref.

5.4 Where should state live?

Each piece of state should live in the lowest component that contains every component that needs it. If only the cart uses the lines, they live in the cart. If the header also needs the number of items, the lines move up to the component that contains both, and travel down as props, with events coming back up. If many distant parts of the app need it, Chapter 9 gives it a store of its own.

Quick check

  1. Why did changing the coupon print nothing in reactivity.ts?
  2. What is wrong with const { stock } = product if product is reactive?
  3. Why is the number of items in the cart a computed, not a ref?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

5.5 Watchers: reacting to changes

A computed calculates a value. Sometimes a change must instead do something outside Vue’s world: start a timer, save to storage, call a server, change the page’s title. That is the job of a watcher:

Timer.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { onWatcherCleanup, ref, watch } from "vue";

const running = ref(false);
const seconds = ref(0);

watch(running, (on) => {
  if (!on) return;
  const id = setInterval(() => seconds.value++, 1000);
  // Runs before the next run of the watcher, and when the component is removed
  onWatcherCleanup(() => clearInterval(id));
});
</script>

<template>
  <p>
    <strong>{{ seconds }}</strong> seconds
    <button type="button" :aria-pressed="running" @click="running = !running">
      {{ running ? "Stop" : "Start" }}
    </button>
  </p>
</template>

watch(running, callback) calls the callback every time running changes. The important line is onWatcherCleanup. It registers a function that Vue calls before the next run of the watcher, and when the component is removed from the page. Without it, pressing “Stop” would leave the interval running forever, and pressing “Start” again would start a second one, so the seconds would count twice as fast.

Vue has two kinds of watcher:

  • watch(source, callback) watches what you name, and gives you the new and old values. It does not run at first unless you pass { immediate: true }. To notice changes deep inside objects and arrays, pass { deep: true }.
  • watchEffect(fn) runs fn at once, tracks everything it reads, and runs again when any of it changes, like the effect of section 5.1.

Watch out: you might not need a watcher

A watcher that copies data into another ref, such as watch(lines, () => { count.value = … }), is a computed value written the hard way, and it is briefly wrong between the change and the watcher. If you are calculating, use computed. Use a watcher only when a change must cause something outside the data.

The life of a component

A component is created, put into the page (mounted), updated as its data changes, and finally removed (unmounted). Functions such as onMounted and onUnmounted run code at those moments: onMounted is the first moment the component’s elements exist in the page, so it is where you measure them, focus a field, or start a library that needs a real element. Clean up in onUnmounted anything you started in onMounted. And to reach an element itself, give it ref="name" in the template and read it with useTemplateRef("name"), as the tabs project of Chapter 4 did.

5.6 v-model on your own components

On an input, v-model="text" keeps a ref and the field in step, in both directions. Your own components can support v-model too. defineModel creates a ref that reads the prop the parent passed and, when you change it, emits the event that updates the parent:

QuantityInput.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
// defineModel: a prop and an event in one ref, for v-model
const quantity = defineModel<number>({ required: true });
const props = defineProps<{ max: number; label: string }>();
</script>

<template>
  <div class="quantity" role="group" :aria-label="label">
    <button type="button" class="secondary" :disabled="quantity <= 1" aria-label="−1" @click="quantity--">−</button>
    <output>{{ quantity }}</output>
    <button type="button" class="secondary" :disabled="quantity >= props.max" aria-label="+1" @click="quantity++">+</button>
  </div>
</template>
Order.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { ref } from "vue";
import QuantityInput from "./QuantityInput.vue";

const mugs = ref(1);
</script>

<template>
  <p>Bit mug</p>
  <QuantityInput v-model="mugs" :max="5" label="Quantity" />
  <p>You ordered: {{ mugs }} · Total: {{ mugs * 8 }} EGP</p>
</template>

This does not break the rule “data flows down, events flow up”: v-model="mugs" is short for :modelValue="mugs" @update:modelValue="mugs = $event". The parent still owns the number; it just lets the child ask for changes with one short attribute.

Professional habit: name what a watcher is for

Every watcher is a small piece of behaviour that runs at times you do not see. Keep each one short, put a comment above it that says why it exists, and clean up whatever it starts. When a component has more than two or three, the next chapter shows how to move them into composables with names.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Destructuring a reactive object or a propA copy that never updatesRead obj.prop where needed, or use toRefs
let state = reactive({…}), then state = {…}The screen keeps showing the old objectUse a ref and assign .value
Storing values that can be calculatedCopies that drift apartcomputed
A watcher that only sets another refA value that is briefly wrong, and more codecomputed
Starting a timer or request without cleaning upDoubled timers, answers arriving lateonWatcherCleanup or onUnmounted
Reading the DOM right after changing dataThe old page is measuredawait nextTick() first

Summary

  • Vue tracks which code reads which data, and triggers exactly that code when the data changes.
  • reactive wraps objects in a proxy; ref wraps any value in an object with .value. Prefer ref.
  • Destructuring copies values and loses reactivity; toRefs keeps the link. Updates are batched; nextTick waits for the page.
  • Keep state minimal; calculate the rest with computed. Put state in the lowest component that contains all its users.
  • Watchers are for effects outside the data. Clean up with onWatcherCleanup; use onMounted and onUnmounted for the life of the component.
  • defineModel gives your components v-model, without breaking one-way data flow.

Quick check answers: 1. Because total never read coupon, so Vue tracked no link between them. 2. It copies the current number into a plain variable, which does not change when the product does. 3. Because it can be calculated from the lines; storing it would create a second copy that must be updated by hand.

Practice projects

Before each project, write down every piece of state you plan to keep, then cross out each one that can be calculated from the others. What remains goes in refs; the rest becomes computed.

Project 5.1 · Easy

A counter with steps and undo

A counter that adds or subtracts a chosen step, shows its history, and can undo any number of changes.

You will practise: minimal state, computed, changing arrays in place, and v-model.number.

  1. Keep only two refs: the step, and the history of values, starting with [0].
  2. Make the current value a computed: the last item of the history.
  3. Each button pushes a new value onto the history; “Undo” pops one, and is disabled when only the first value is left.
  4. Bind the step with v-model.number, so it is a number, not text.
  5. Give the value aria-live="polite", so screen readers announce the new number.

Hint: because the history is the state, undo is one line. Had you stored the value and changed it with +=, undo would need a second list, kept in step with the first by hand.

History.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { computed, ref } from "vue";

const step = ref(1);
const history = ref<number[]>([0]);
const value = computed(() => history.value.at(-1) ?? 0);

function change(by: number) {
  history.value.push(value.value + by);
}

function undo() {
  if (history.value.length > 1) history.value.pop();
}
</script>

<template>
  <p class="value" aria-live="polite">{{ value }}</p>
  <div class="row">
    <button type="button" class="secondary" @click="change(-step)">−{{ step }}</button>
    <button type="button" @click="change(step)">+{{ step }}</button>
    <button type="button" class="secondary" :disabled="history.length === 1" @click="undo">Undo</button>
  </div>
  <label>Step <input id="step" v-model.number="step" type="number" min="1" max="10" /></label>
  <p class="muted">History: {{ history.join(" → ") }}</p>
</template>

Project 5.2 · Medium

A task list that remembers

A task list that saves itself in the browser, and comes back after a reload, safely.

You will practise: v-model on text and checkboxes, a deep watcher, localStorage, and defensive loading.

  1. Load the tasks once, when the component starts, inside a try: saved data can be missing, broken or from an older version.
  2. Bind the new task’s text with v-model, and add it on @submit.prevent, so Enter works and the page does not reload.
  3. Bind each checkbox straight to task.done with v-model: Vue changes the object in place.
  4. Save with watch(tasks, save, { deep: true }). Without deep, ticking a box would not be noticed, because the array itself did not change.
  5. Show how many tasks are left with a computed, and add a button that removes the done ones.

Hint: deep: true walks the whole structure on every change. That is fine for a task list; for thousands of items, save less often, or watch a smaller part.

Project 5.3 · Challenge

Search as you type, without races

Search products while the user types, and never show the results of an old query, even when a slow answer arrives after a fast one.

You will practise: watchers with async work, onWatcherCleanup, AbortController, and loading states announced to screen readers.

  1. Use the pretend API below. Like a real search, it answers short queries more slowly, so “b” can arrive after “bit”.
  2. Watch the query. For an empty query, clear the results and stop.
  3. For each search, create an AbortController, and register controller.abort() with onWatcherCleanup. The next keystroke cancels the previous request.
  4. Await the results inside try; an aborted request rejects, and the catch simply ignores it.
  5. Show “Searching for…” and the number of results with role="status".

Hint: type “bit” quickly with and without the cleanup line. Without it, you will sometimes see the results for “b” replace the results for “bit”: a race condition, one of the most common bugs in real apps.

fake-api.ts
import { catalogue, type Product } from "./cart-model";

// A pretend server: answers after a delay, slower for short queries, like a real search.
export function searchProducts(query: string, signal: AbortSignal): Promise<Product[]> {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const timer = setTimeout(() => {
      const q = query.trim().toLowerCase();
      resolve(catalogue.filter((p) => p.name.toLowerCase().includes(q)));
    }, 600 - query.length * 100);
    signal.addEventListener("abort", () => {
      clearTimeout(timer);
      reject(signal.reason);
    });
  });
}
Search.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { onWatcherCleanup, ref, watch } from "vue";
import { searchProducts } from "../fake-api";
import type { Product } from "../cart-model";

const query = ref("");
const results = ref<Product[]>([]);
const loading = ref(false);

watch(query, async (q) => {
  if (!q.trim()) {
    results.value = [];
    return;
  }
  // If the user types again, cancel the old request
  const controller = new AbortController();
  onWatcherCleanup(() => controller.abort());
  loading.value = true;
  try {
    results.value = await searchProducts(q, controller.signal);
    loading.value = false;
  } catch {
    // aborted: a newer search is already running
  }
});
</script>

<template>
  <label for="q">Search products</label>
  <input id="q" v-model="query" type="search" autocomplete="off" />
  <p v-if="loading" class="muted" role="status">Searching for “{{ query }}”…</p>
  <template v-else-if="query">
    <p role="status">{{ results.length }} results</p>
    <ul>
      <li v-for="p in results" :key="p.id">{{ p.name }}</li>
    </ul>
  </template>
</template>

Exercises

  1. Easy In Cart.vue, add a “Clear” button that empties the cart. Which of the computed values do you need to change?
  2. Easy Predict, then check, what reactivity.ts prints if you add cart.lines.pop() at the end.
  3. Medium Give Timer.vue a “Reset” button, and make the page title show the seconds while the timer runs, with watchEffect. What should the title be when it stops?
  4. Medium Give QuantityInput a second model, v-model:note, for a gift note, with defineModel("note").
  5. Challenge Write a watch that saves the cart’s lines to localStorage, and load them when the component starts. What happens if the saved data is from an older version with a different shape, and how do you protect against it?

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 6

The timer, the saved tasks and the search all mix several refs, a watcher and a cleanup. The next chapter moves logic like that out of components into composables: plain functions that any component can reuse, and that you can test on their own.

6

Chapter 6

Composables: Reusing Logic

In this chapter you will learn to

  • move stateful logic out of components into composables, and give it a name
  • decide whether each caller gets its own state or everyone shares one
  • accept values, refs and getters as inputs, with toValue
  • write async composables that cancel stale work, and composables that clean up after themselves
  • use VueUse instead of writing common composables yourself
  • test composables without any component

Components are good at reusing pieces of the screen. But much of the code in a component is not about the screen: a timer, a search that cancels old requests, the state of the network, a value saved in the browser. When two components need the same behaviour, copying it means fixing every bug twice. Vue’s answer is the composable: a plain function that uses the Composition API, keeps its own reactive state, and returns it. It is the main way Vue apps share logic, and the idea behind most of the Vue ecosystem.

Bit

The rule of thumb I use: when a <script setup> needs a comment to explain what a group of refs and watchers are doing together, that group wants to be a composable with a name.

6.1 A first composable

Here is the timer of Chapter 5, moved out of its component and given more abilities. By convention, a composable’s name starts with use:

composables/useStopwatch.ts
import { onScopeDispose, readonly, ref } from "vue";

// A composable: a function that uses the Composition API and returns reactive state
export function useStopwatch() {
  // Each call makes new refs: two components never share a stopwatch by accident
  const seconds = ref(0);
  const running = ref(false);
  let timer: ReturnType<typeof setInterval> | undefined;

  function start() {
    if (running.value) return;
    running.value = true;
    timer = setInterval(() => seconds.value++, 1000);
  }
  function stop() {
    clearInterval(timer);
    running.value = false;
  }
  function reset() {
    stop();
    seconds.value = 0;
  }

  onScopeDispose(stop);
  return { seconds: readonly(seconds), running: readonly(running), start, stop, reset };
}
Stopwatch.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { useStopwatch } from "./composables/useStopwatch";

const { seconds, running, start, stop, reset } = useStopwatch();
</script>

<template>
  <p>
    <strong>{{ seconds }}</strong> seconds
    <button v-if="!running" type="button" @click="start">Start</button>
    <button v-else type="button" @click="stop">Stop</button>
    <button type="button" class="secondary" @click="reset">Reset</button>
  </p>
</template>

The component is now a short description of the screen. Everything about time lives in one tested function, and any other component can have a stopwatch with one line. Two details matter:

  • readonly on the returned refs means callers can read seconds but not set it. The only ways to change the stopwatch are its own functions, so it can never end up in a strange state.
  • onScopeDispose(stop) registers cleanup with whatever is running the composable. Inside a component, that happens when the component is removed, so a stopwatch never keeps ticking on a screen that is gone.

Watch out: a computed sees only reactive reads

The first version of this composable had const running = computed(() => timer !== undefined && …), where timer was a plain let. Vue cannot track a plain variable, so the computed sometimes kept an old answer, and the screen showed “Start” on a running stopwatch. Anything a computed or a template depends on must be reactive. A plain let is fine only for values the screen never shows, like the interval’s id here.

6.2 Own state or shared state?

Because a composable is just a function, where you create the refs decides who shares them:

composables/counters.ts
import { ref } from "vue";

export function useCounter() {
  const count = ref(0); // Each call makes new refs: two components never share a stopwatch by accident
  return { count, add: () => count.value++ };
}

const visitors = ref(0); // Created once, outside the function: everyone who calls it shares the same value
export function useVisitors() {
  return { visitors, arrive: () => visitors.value++ };
}
state.ts
import { useCounter, useVisitors } from "./composables/counters";

const a = useCounter();
const b = useCounter();
a.add();
a.add();
b.add();
console.log("First call:", a.count.value, "Second call:", b.count.value);

useVisitors().arrive();
useVisitors().arrive();
console.log("Shared by every call:", useVisitors().visitors.value);
Terminal
First call: 2 Second call: 1
Shared by every call: 2

Refs created inside the function are new for every call: the usual case. Refs created outside, at the top of the module, exist once, so every caller shares them. That is a quick way to share state, but use it with care: it is shared by the whole app, it is hard to reset in tests, and in server rendering (Chapter 17) it would even be shared between different users’ requests. For real app-wide state, Chapter 9 introduces Pinia.

6.3 Inputs: values, refs or getters

A useful composable accepts its inputs in whatever form the caller has them. MaybeRefOrGetter<T> means “a plain value, a ref, or a function that returns the value”, and toValue reads any of them. Watching () => toValue(source) then reacts to changes, whichever form was passed:

composables/useDebounced.ts
import { ref, toValue, watch, type MaybeRefOrGetter, type Ref } from "vue";

// Accepts a value, a ref or a getter, so callers can pass whatever they have
export function useDebounced<T>(source: MaybeRefOrGetter<T>, ms = 300): Readonly<Ref<T>> {
  const value = ref(toValue(source)) as Ref<T>;
  watch(
    () => toValue(source),
    (now, _before, onCleanup) => {
      const id = setTimeout(() => (value.value = now), ms);
      onCleanup(() => clearTimeout(id));
    },
  );
  return value;
}

useDebounced returns a copy of a value that changes only after the original has stopped changing for a while. It is what you want between a search box and a server: the user types “bit”, and the server is asked once, not three times. The cleanup cancels the waiting timer each time a new letter arrives.

6.4 An async composable

Composables shine for asynchronous work, because all the hard parts can be solved once: loading and error states, and above all, ignoring answers to questions nobody is asking any more:

products.ts
export type Product = { id: number; name: string; price: number };

const catalogue: Product[] = [
  { id: 1, name: "Code from Zero", price: 25 },
  { id: 2, name: "Bit mug", price: 8 },
  { id: 3, name: "Bit cap", price: 12 },
  { id: 4, name: "Bit sticker", price: 3 },
];

// A pretend server: answers after a delay, and can be cancelled like fetch
export function searchProducts(query: string, signal: AbortSignal): Promise<Product[]> {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const timer = setTimeout(() => {
      const q = query.trim().toLowerCase();
      resolve(catalogue.filter((p) => p.name.toLowerCase().includes(q)));
    }, 400);
    signal.addEventListener("abort", () => {
      clearTimeout(timer);
      reject(signal.reason);
    });
  });
}
composables/useSearch.ts
import { ref, toValue, watch, type MaybeRefOrGetter } from "vue";
import { searchProducts, type Product } from "../products";

export function useSearch(query: MaybeRefOrGetter<string>) {
  const results = ref<Product[]>([]);
  const loading = ref(false);
  const error = ref<string | null>(null);

  watch(
    () => toValue(query).trim(),
    async (q, _before, onCleanup) => {
      error.value = null;
      if (!q) {
        results.value = [];
        return;
      }
      // A new query cancels the request for the old one
      const controller = new AbortController();
      onCleanup(() => controller.abort());
      loading.value = true;
      try {
        results.value = await searchProducts(q, controller.signal);
      } catch (e) {
        if (controller.signal.aborted) return;
        error.value = (e as Error).message;
      }
      loading.value = false;
    },
    { immediate: true },
  );

  return { results, loading, error };
}

The third argument of a watch callback, onCleanup, works like onWatcherCleanup: before the watcher runs for a new query, the old request is aborted. An aborted request is not an error, so the catch checks for that and stays quiet. Composables also compose: the page passes the debounced query to the search, and neither composable knows about the other.

Search.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { ref } from "vue";
import { useDebounced } from "./composables/useDebounced";
import { useSearch } from "./composables/useSearch";

const query = ref("");
const { results, loading, error } = useSearch(useDebounced(query, 250));
</script>

<template>
  <label for="q">Search products</label>
  <input id="q" v-model="query" type="search" autocomplete="off" />
  <p v-if="loading" role="status" class="muted">Searching…</p>
  <p v-else-if="error" role="alert">Could not search: {{ error }}</p>
  <template v-else-if="query">
    <p role="status">{{ results.length }} results</p>
    <ul>
      <li v-for="p in results" :key="p.id">{{ p.name }}</li>
    </ul>
  </template>
</template>

Quick check

  1. Two components call useStopwatch(). Do they share the seconds? Why?
  2. What three kinds of input does MaybeRefOrGetter<string> accept?
  3. Why is an aborted request not shown as an error?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

6.5 Composables that clean up after themselves

Listening to the browser is a classic source of leaks: a listener added in a component and never removed keeps running, and keeps the component in memory, long after the user has left the screen. A small composable makes the mistake impossible:

composables/useEventListener.ts
import { onScopeDispose } from "vue";

// Removed when the component (or any effect scope) that called it goes away
export function useEventListener<K extends keyof WindowEventMap>(
  target: Window,
  event: K,
  handler: (e: WindowEventMap[K]) => void,
) {
  target.addEventListener(event, handler);
  onScopeDispose(() => target.removeEventListener(event, handler));
}
composables/useOnline.ts
import { ref } from "vue";
import { useEventListener } from "./useEventListener";

export function useOnline() {
  const online = ref(navigator.onLine);
  useEventListener(window, "online", () => (online.value = true));
  useEventListener(window, "offline", () => (online.value = false));
  return online;
}
Status.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { ref } from "vue";
import { useEventListener } from "./composables/useEventListener";
import { useOnline } from "./composables/useOnline";

const online = useOnline();
const width = ref(window.innerWidth);
useEventListener(window, "resize", () => (width.value = window.innerWidth));
</script>

<template>
  <p :class="online ? 'ok' : 'warn'" role="status">{{ online ? "Online" : "Offline: changes will be saved when you are back" }}</p>
  <p>Window width: {{ width }}px</p>
</template>

Notice that useOnline is built on useEventListener. Small composables become the building blocks of bigger ones, the same way small components do.

Watch out: call composables at the top of <script setup>

A composable registers its watchers and cleanup with the component that is being set up right now. Called later, inside a click handler or after an await, there is no such component: the watchers are never stopped and onScopeDispose warns. Call composables at the top level, synchronously, and use the functions they return later.

6.6 VueUse

Many composables are needed by almost every app: reading and saving localStorage, media queries, the size of an element, the clipboard, the page’s visibility. VueUse is a free library of more than two hundred of them, well tested, typed, and with the cleanup already done. Here is a dark-theme switch that is saved in the browser and stays in step across open tabs:

Theme.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { useLocalStorage } from "@vueuse/core";
import { watchEffect } from "vue";

// The same idea from VueUse: saved in localStorage, kept in step across tabs
const dark = useLocalStorage("dark", false);
watchEffect(() => document.documentElement.classList.toggle("dark", dark.value));
</script>

<template>
  <button type="button" :aria-pressed="dark" @click="dark = !dark">Dark theme</button>
</template>

Before writing a composable for something general, look in VueUse. Write your own for what is specific to your app: its data, its rules, its server.

6.7 Testing composables

Because composables are functions, they can be tested without rendering anything. Two tools make it easy: Vitest’s fake timers, which move time forward instantly, and effectScope, which plays the role of a component, so cleanup runs when the test calls scope.stop():

composables/useStopwatch.test.ts
import { effectScope } from "vue";
import { afterEach, describe, expect, it, vi } from "vitest";
import { useStopwatch } from "./useStopwatch";

afterEach(() => vi.useRealTimers());

describe("useStopwatch", () => {
  it("counts seconds only while running", () => {
    vi.useFakeTimers();
    const scope = effectScope();
    const watch = scope.run(() => useStopwatch())!;
    watch.start();
    vi.advanceTimersByTime(3000);
    watch.stop();
    vi.advanceTimersByTime(5000);
    expect(watch.seconds.value).toBe(3);
    scope.stop();
  });

  it("reset stops and returns to zero", () => {
    vi.useFakeTimers();
    const scope = effectScope();
    const watch = scope.run(() => useStopwatch())!;
    watch.start();
    vi.advanceTimersByTime(2000);
    watch.reset();
    expect(watch.seconds.value).toBe(0);
    expect(watch.running.value).toBe(false);
    scope.stop();
  });
});
composables/useDebounced.test.ts
import { nextTick, ref } from "vue";
import { expect, it, vi } from "vitest";
import { useDebounced } from "./useDebounced";

it("waits until the value has stopped changing", async () => {
  vi.useFakeTimers();
  const text = ref("b");
  const slow = useDebounced(text, 300);
  for (const next of ["bi", "bit"]) {
    text.value = next;
    await nextTick();
    vi.advanceTimersByTime(100);
  }
  expect(slow.value).toBe("b");
  vi.advanceTimersByTime(300);
  expect(slow.value).toBe("bit");
  vi.useRealTimers();
});
Terminal
$ npx vitest run --reporter=verbose
 RUN  v5.1.1 .
 ✓ composables/useStopwatch.test.ts > useStopwatch > counts seconds only while running 5ms
 ✓ composables/useStopwatch.test.ts > useStopwatch > reset stops and returns to zero 1ms
 ✓ projects/usePagination.test.ts > splits a list into pages 3ms
 ✓ projects/usePagination.test.ts > never goes past the last page 1ms
 ✓ composables/useDebounced.test.ts > waits until the value has stopped changing 8ms
 Test Files  3 passed (3)
      Tests  5 passed (5)
   Duration  1.11s (environment 80%, import 14%, transform 5%, tests 1%, worker 1%)

Five seconds of stopwatch and a third of a second of typing, tested in a few milliseconds. Logic that lived in a component could only be tested by rendering the component and clicking it; as a composable, it is tested directly.

Professional habit: a composable has one job

Name each composable after the one thing it does: useStopwatch, useSearch, useOnline. When a name needs “and”, split it. Return only what callers need, as readonly where they should not write, and document what it cleans up.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Refs at module level by accidentEvery component shares one stateCreate refs inside the function
A computed that reads a plain variableA value that does not updateMake the variable a ref
Calling a composable in a handler or after awaitWatchers that never stop; a warningCall it at the top of <script setup>
Listeners and timers without cleanupLeaks and code running for removed screensonScopeDispose, or VueUse
Destructuring a returned reactive objectValues that stop updatingReturn an object of refs
Accepting only plain valuesCallers must unwrap refs, and lose reactivityMaybeRefOrGetter and toValue

Summary

  • A composable is a use… function that uses the Composition API and returns reactive state and functions.
  • Refs created inside are per call; refs at module level are shared by the whole app.
  • Accept inputs as MaybeRefOrGetter and read them with toValue. Return readonly refs where callers should not write.
  • Async composables handle loading, errors and cancel stale work in their cleanup.
  • Register cleanup with onScopeDispose, and call composables synchronously in <script setup>.
  • Use VueUse for general needs, and test composables directly, with fake timers and effectScope.

Quick check answers: 1. No: seconds is created inside the function, so each call makes its own ref. 2. A plain string, a ref containing a string, and a function that returns a string. 3. Because the user did nothing wrong: the request was cancelled on purpose, because a newer one replaced it.

Practice projects

For each composable, write its signature and a test before its body: what it takes, what it returns, and what it cleans up. If the test is hard to write, the design is usually wrong.

Project 6.1 · Easy

Your own useStoredRef

Write the composable behind VueUse’s useLocalStorage yourself: a ref whose value is saved in the browser, survives reloads, and never crashes on bad data.

You will practise: a generic composable, watch with deep, defensive parsing, and the storage event.

  1. Write useStoredRef<T>(key: string, initial: T). Read the saved JSON inside try, and fall back to initial if it is missing or broken.
  2. Save on every change with watch(value, save, { deep: true }).
  3. Listen to the window’s storage event, which fires when another tab changes the same key, and update the ref, using your useEventListener so it is cleaned up.
  4. Use it for the tasks of Chapter 5, and check in two tabs that ticking a task in one updates the other.

Hint: after writing it, compare with VueUse’s source code. Reading how a well-maintained library solves the same problem, and which cases it handles that you missed, is one of the fastest ways to improve.

Project 6.2 · Medium

A tested usePagination

A composable that splits any list into pages, follows the list when it changes, and never shows an empty page.

You will practise: generic types, MaybeRefOrGetter, computed values that depend on each other, and tests written first.

  1. Write the tests first: seven items in pages of three give three pages, and next() shows items 4 to 6.
  2. Write a harder test: go to page 3, then shrink the list to two items. The current page must become 1, not stay on an empty page 3.
  3. Keep only the requested page in a ref. Make the number of pages, the current page (the requested one, clamped) and the rows computed.
  4. Use it in the catalogue of Chapter 4 with “Previous” and “Next” buttons, disabled at the ends, and “Page 2 of 3” between them.

Hint: clamping in a computed, instead of correcting the ref in a watcher, means the page can never be wrong, not even for a moment.

usePagination.ts
import { computed, ref, toValue, type MaybeRefOrGetter } from "vue";

export function usePagination<T>(items: MaybeRefOrGetter<readonly T[]>, size = 3) {
  const page = ref(1);
  const pages = computed(() => Math.max(1, Math.ceil(toValue(items).length / size)));
  const current = computed(() => Math.min(page.value, pages.value));
  const rows = computed(() => toValue(items).slice((current.value - 1) * size, current.value * size));
  return {
    page: current,
    pages,
    rows,
    next: () => (page.value = Math.min(current.value + 1, pages.value)),
    previous: () => (page.value = Math.max(current.value - 1, 1)),
  };
}
usePagination.test.ts
import { ref } from "vue";
import { expect, it } from "vitest";
import { usePagination } from "./usePagination";

it("splits a list into pages", () => {
  const p = usePagination([1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7], 3);
  expect(p.pages.value).toBe(3);
  p.next();
  expect(p.rows.value).toEqual([4, 5, 6]);
});

it("never goes past the last page", () => {
  const list = ref([1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7]);
  const p = usePagination(list, 3);
  p.next();
  p.next();
  p.next();
  expect(p.page.value).toBe(3);
  list.value = [1, 2];
  expect(p.page.value).toBe(1);
  expect(p.rows.value).toEqual([1, 2]);
});
Terminal
$ npx vitest run --reporter=verbose projects
 RUN  v5.1.1 .

 Test Files  1 passed (1)

Project 6.3 · Challenge

An endless list

A product list that loads the next page by itself when the user scrolls near the end, with a button as a fallback, and without loading the same page twice.

You will practise: IntersectionObserver, template refs passed to composables, async state, and accessible loading.

  1. Write useIntersection(target, callback), where target is a template ref. Create the observer when the element exists, and disconnect it in the cleanup.
  2. Write useInfiniteList(loadPage), returning items, loading, done and loadMore(). loadMore does nothing while a page is loading or when the last page has arrived.
  3. Put an empty element after the list with ref="end", and call loadMore when it becomes visible.
  4. Also show a “Load more” button: keyboard users and screen readers may never trigger the observer.
  5. Announce “Loaded 10 more products” in a role="status" element after each page.

Hint: the most common bug is two pages loading at once, because the end marker stays visible while the first request is running. The loading check at the top of loadMore is the whole fix; write a test for it.

Exercises

  1. Easy Add a laps list and a lap() function to useStopwatch, which records the current seconds. Return the list as readonly.
  2. Easy Write useToggle(initial = false), returning a ref and a toggle function, and use it for the theme button.
  3. Medium Write useMediaQuery(query) with matchMedia and useEventListener-style cleanup, and show “wide” or “narrow” with "(min-width: 600px)".
  4. Medium Write a test for useSearch with fake timers: type “b”, then “bit” 100 ms later, and check that only the results for “bit” arrive.
  5. Challenge Turn useSearch into a general useAsync(fn, input) that runs any cancellable function when the input changes, and rewrite useSearch with it in three lines.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 7

Real apps have many screens and talk to real servers. The next chapter adds Vue Router for addresses and navigation, and TanStack Query, a library of composables that handles server data, caching and refetching better than anything we could write by hand.

7

Chapter 7

Vue Router and Server Data

In this chapter you will learn to

  • give every screen of an app its own address with Vue Router
  • share a layout between pages with nested routes and RouterView
  • read route parameters as typed props, and keep filters in the address so they can be shared
  • load server data with TanStack Query: caching, loading and error states, retries
  • change server data with mutations, including optimistic updates that roll back on failure
  • protect pages with navigation guards, and load each page only when it is needed

A real app is many screens: a list, a details page, a basket, a profile. Users expect each one to have an address they can bookmark, share and reach with the back button. And almost every screen shows data that lives on a server, which arrives late, sometimes fails, and changes while the user looks at it. This chapter adds the two libraries that most Vue teams use for these jobs: Vue Router, Vue’s official router, and TanStack Query for server data.

Bit

In the last chapter you wrote useSearch, with cancellation and loading and error states. TanStack Query is a set of composables that does that work, and much more, for every request in the app. Now you know what it does inside.

7.1 Routes

An app with a router is still one HTML page, a single-page application. The router reads the address, decides which components to show, and, when the user clicks a link, changes the address without reloading the page. The routes are plain data: a tree of objects, each with a path and a component:

routes.ts
import type { RouteRecordRaw } from "vue-router";
import Layout from "./views/Layout.vue";
import Home from "./views/Home.vue";
import Products from "./views/Products.vue";
import Product from "./views/Product.vue";
import NotFound from "./views/NotFound.vue";

// Each route maps an address to a component; children are drawn inside their parent's RouterView
export const routes: RouteRecordRaw[] = [
  {
    path: "/",
    component: Layout,
    children: [
      { path: "", component: Home },
      {
        path: "products",
        children: [
          { path: "", component: Products },
          // Pass the id as a typed prop, so the component does not need to know about the router
          { path: ":id(\\d+)", component: Product, props: (route) => ({ id: Number(route.params.id) }) },
        ],
      },
      { path: ":path(.*)*", component: NotFound },
    ],
  },
];
  • The route / shows Layout. Its children are drawn inside it, where Layout places <RouterView />. The navigation bar is written once and every page gets it. These are nested routes. A child with an empty path is shown at exactly its parent’s address.
  • :id(\d+) is a route parameter that matches digits only, so /products/2 matches and /products/abc does not. Parameters are strings, so the props function turns the id into a number and passes it as a normal prop. The component never touches the router, which makes it easy to reuse and to test.
  • :path(.*)* matches anything the other routes did not, so mistyped addresses get a friendly page instead of an empty screen.
views/Layout.vue
<template>
  <div class="layout">
    <nav>
      <span class="brand">Bit's shop</span>
      <RouterLink to="/" exact-active-class="router-link-active" active-class="">Home</RouterLink>
      <RouterLink to="/products">Products</RouterLink>
    </nav>
    <RouterView />
  </div>
</template>

The app starts in main.ts, which installs the router and TanStack Query as plugins, so every component can use them:

main.ts
import { createApp } from "vue";
import { createRouter, createWebHistory } from "vue-router";
import { VueQueryPlugin } from "@tanstack/vue-query";
import App from "./App.vue";
import { routes } from "./routes";

const router = createRouter({ history: createWebHistory(), routes });

createApp(App)
  .use(router)
  .use(VueQueryPlugin, { queryClientConfig: { defaultOptions: { queries: { staleTime: 30_000 } } } })
  .mount("#app");

About the screenshots

createWebHistory reads the real address of the page. The screenshots in this chapter are taken from files, not from a site, so they start the same routes with createMemoryHistory, which keeps its address in memory, and then click the links exactly as a user would.

7.2 Links and navigation

Inside the app, never write <a href> for your own pages: it would reload the whole page and lose all state. Use <RouterLink to="/products">, which changes the address and redraws only what changed. A RouterLink also knows whether it points to the current page: it adds the class router-link-active when its route is part of the current one, and router-link-exact-active when it is exactly the current one, so the navigation bar can highlight it. In code, after saving a form for example, const router = useRouter() and then router.push("/products") move the user.

The address is state too

Look at how the product list filters by category. It has no ref for the filter: the category lives in the address, as a query parameter, /products?category=gifts, read from useRoute().query. The filter links point to { query: { category: "gifts" } }. This costs nothing and gives users three things for free: they can share the filtered list, bookmark it, and use the back button to undo a filter. Search text, sorting, the current page of results and the selected tab are all good candidates for the address.

views/Products.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { computed } from "vue";
import { useRoute } from "vue-router";
import { useQuery } from "@tanstack/vue-query";
import { getProducts } from "../api";
import Skeleton from "./Skeleton.vue";

const CATEGORIES = [
  { value: null, label: "All" },
  { value: "books", label: "Books" },
  { value: "gifts", label: "Gifts" },
];

const route = useRoute();
const category = computed(() => (typeof route.query.category === "string" ? route.query.category : null));

// The key names the data: a computed category in it refetches when the address changes
const query = useQuery({
  queryKey: ["products", category],
  queryFn: () => getProducts(category.value),
});
</script>

<template>
  <section class="card">
    <div class="filters">
      <RouterLink
        v-for="c in CATEGORIES"
        :key="c.label"
        :to="{ query: c.value ? { category: c.value } : {} }"
        :class="{ on: c.value === category }"
      >
        {{ c.label }}
      </RouterLink>
    </div>
    <Skeleton v-if="query.isPending.value" />
    <p v-else-if="query.isError.value" class="error">Something went wrong: {{ query.error.value?.message }}</p>
    <ul v-else class="list">
      <li v-for="p in query.data.value" :key="p.id">
        <RouterLink :to="`/products/${p.id}`">{{ p.name }}</RouterLink>
        <span class="price">{{ p.price }} EGP</span>
      </li>
    </ul>
  </section>
</template>

7.3 Server data with TanStack Query

Data from a server is not like state that your app owns. Someone else can change it at any time, you only ever have a copy, and getting that copy takes time and can fail. A composable like useSearch works for one screen, but a real app soon needs much more:

  • A cache, so going back to a page shows it instantly instead of loading again.
  • Deduplication, so two components asking for the same data make one request.
  • Retries when the network fails for a moment.
  • Refreshing stale data when the user comes back to the tab.
  • Protection from the race between old and new requests.

TanStack Query provides all of these through one composable. You give useQuery a query key, an array that names the data, and a query function that returns a promise:

  • The key is a name in a cache: ["products", "gifts"] and ["products", null] are cached separately. In Vue, the key can contain refs and computed values; ["products", category] is reactive. When the address changes, category changes, the key changes, and the right data is loaded, or shown at once if it is already cached.
  • The result holds refs: isPending, isError with error, and data, which TypeScript knows is a Product[]. These are the four states of Chapter 4, ready-made. Templates only unwrap refs at the top level, which is why Products.vue writes query.isPending.value, while Product.vue destructures the result and writes isPending.
  • staleTime: 30_000 in main.ts says data stays fresh for 30 seconds; inside that time, visiting the page again makes no request at all.
views/Product.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { useQuery } from "@tanstack/vue-query";
import { getProduct, NotFoundError } from "../api";
import Skeleton from "./Skeleton.vue";

const props = defineProps<{ id: number }>();

const { data, error, isPending } = useQuery({
  queryKey: ["product", () => props.id],
  queryFn: () => getProduct(props.id),
  // Retry network errors twice, but never a product that does not exist
  retry: (count, e) => !(e instanceof NotFoundError) && count < 2,
});
</script>

<template>
  <Skeleton v-if="isPending" />
  <p v-else-if="error" class="card error">
    {{ error instanceof NotFoundError ? "There is no product with this number." : error.message }}
  </p>
  <article v-else-if="data" class="card">
    <h2>{{ data.name }}</h2>
    <p>{{ data.description }}</p>
    <p class="price">{{ data.price }} EGP</p>
    <RouterLink to="/products">← Back to products</RouterLink>
  </article>
</template>

The details page receives id as a prop and puts a getter, () => props.id, in its key, so moving from one product to another refetches. It uses retry to try twice on network errors, but never on NotFoundError: retrying a product that does not exist is pointless.

Tip: skeletons instead of spinners

A skeleton that has roughly the shape of the coming content feels faster than a spinner, and stops the layout from jumping. Mark it with aria-busy="true" and a label, so screen readers know that content is on its way.

Quick check

  1. Why should an app use <RouterLink> instead of <a href> for its own pages?
  2. What happens at the address /products/abc with the routes of this chapter?
  3. Two components call useQuery with the key ["product", 2] at the same time. How many requests are sent?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

7.4 Changing server data

Reading uses queries; writing uses mutations. useMutation wraps a function that changes data on the server, and gives you mutate to call it, plus isPending and isError for the screen. After a successful change, the cached copies are out of date, so you invalidate them: queryClient.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ["products"] }) marks every query whose key starts with "products" as stale, and the ones on screen reload.

Waiting for the server makes small actions, like a heart or a tick, feel slow. An optimistic update changes the screen at once, assuming the server will agree, and puts the old value back if it does not. Project 7.3 builds one, in three short steps: change the cache and remember the old value; restore it on error; reload the truth from the server either way.

7.5 Guards and lazy routes

Some pages are only for signed-in users. A navigation guard runs before every navigation and can let it pass, cancel it, or send the user elsewhere. Routes carry the information in meta:

router.ts
{ path: "account", component: Account, meta: { requiresAuth: true } }

router.beforeEach((to) => {
  if (to.meta.requiresAuth && !session.signedIn) {
    return { path: "/sign-in", query: { next: to.fullPath } };
  }
});

After signing in, the sign-in page sends the user to route.query.next, back where they wanted to go. Remember that a guard only protects the screen: the server must still check every request, because anyone can call an API without your app (Chapter 14).

Each route is also a natural place to split the code (Chapter 13): users who never open the admin pages should never download them. Give a route a function that imports its component, instead of the component itself:

routes.ts
{ path: "admin", component: () => import("./views/Admin.vue") }

The build puts the admin page in its own file, and the router loads it the first time someone visits the route.

Professional habit: every page needs four states and a way out

For each route, ask: what does the user see while it loads, when it is empty, when it fails, and when the address is wrong? Each answer should offer a way forward: a retry button, a link to the list, a link home. Nobody should ever reach a blank screen.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
<a href="/products"> inside the appThe page reloads and all state is lost<RouterLink to="/products">
Forgetting <RouterView /> in a layoutChild routes never appearPlace it where children should go
Using a route parameter as a number"2" === 2 is falseConvert it in a props function, and constrain it with (\d+)
A query key that misses a variable, or holds category.valueChanging the filter shows the old dataPut the ref or computed itself in the key
Copying query data into a refThe copy goes staleRead data directly, or computed from it
Forgetting to invalidate after a mutationThe screen shows the old datainvalidateQueries in onSettled
Trusting a guard for securityData reachable without the appCheck permissions on the server too

Summary

  • Vue Router maps addresses to components. Routes are data; nested routes share a layout through <RouterView />.
  • Pass route parameters to components as typed props; keep filters and other shareable state in the query of the address.
  • Navigate with RouterLink and router.push; style the current link with router-link-active.
  • TanStack Query caches server data by query key, which can be reactive; it removes duplicate requests, retries and refreshes, and reports pending, error and success.
  • Mutations change server data; invalidate the affected queries afterwards. Optimistic updates change the screen first and roll back on failure.
  • Guards redirect before navigation; lazy routes load each page on its first visit.

Quick check answers: 1. A plain link reloads the whole page and loses the app’s state; RouterLink only changes the address and the parts of the screen that differ. 2. :id(\d+) does not match letters, so the catch-all route shows the “Page not found” message, and no request is made. 3. One: queries with the same key share a single request and a single cache entry.

Practice projects

Start each project from the routes, written as data, before any component. If you can read the whole app from the routes array, the design is right.

Project 7.1 · Easy

A small blog with pretty addresses

A list of posts and a page for each post, at addresses like /posts/rules-for-state, with a kind message for posts that do not exist.

You will practise: nested routes, named routes, route parameters as props, RouterLink, and not-found handling.

  1. Give each post a slug: a short, readable id made from the title, such as keys-explained. Readable addresses are better for users and for search engines.
  2. Write a layout with the blog’s name, a RouterLink to the list, and a RouterView.
  3. Give the routes names, posts and post, and link with { name: "post", params: { slug } }: if an address changes later, no link has to.
  4. Pass the slug as a prop with props: true, and find the post in a computed. If it does not exist, say so and link back home.
  5. Try a wrong address by hand, such as /posts/nothing, and check the message.

Hint: the data here is a constant, so no query is needed. Not every page needs TanStack Query; use it when the data comes from a server.

blog-routes.ts
import type { RouteRecordRaw } from "vue-router";
import BlogLayout from "./BlogLayout.vue";
import PostList from "./PostList.vue";
import PostPage from "./PostPage.vue";

export const routes: RouteRecordRaw[] = [
  {
    path: "/",
    component: BlogLayout,
    children: [
      { path: "", name: "posts", component: PostList },
      { path: "posts/:slug", name: "post", component: PostPage, props: true },
    ],
  },
];

Project 7.2 · Medium

A product list you can share

Extend the shop from this chapter so that the category and a search text live in the address, and every combination can be shared as a link.

You will practise: reading and writing the route’s query, query keys with several reactive parts, and keeping the address in step with an input.

  1. Make category and q computed values from route.query. Both may be missing.
  2. Put both in the query key: ["products", category, q], and pass both to the API function.
  3. Make the search input follow the address: a writable computed whose get reads q, and whose set calls router.replace with the new text, keeping the category.
  4. Use replace, not push, while typing, so every letter does not become a new entry in the back button’s history.
  5. Open the same address in a new tab and check that you see exactly the same list.

Hint: to change one query parameter and keep the others, spread them: router.replace({ query: { ...route.query, q: text || undefined } }). A value of undefined removes the parameter.

Project 7.3 · Challenge

An optimistic favourite button

A heart that fills the moment it is pressed, saves in the background, and quietly puts things back if the server refuses.

You will practise: useMutation, reading and writing the query cache, rolling back with a context value, and invalidating.

  1. Load the product with useQuery under the key ["product", id].
  2. Create a mutation whose mutationFn calls setFavourite(id, value).
  3. In onMutate, cancel queries for the key (so an old answer cannot overwrite you), save the current value with getQueryData, write the new value with setQueryData, and return { previous }.
  4. In onError, write context.previous back. In onSettled, invalidate the key, so the screen ends with the server’s truth.
