  5. Test the failure: the pretend API has a server.failNextSave switch. Watch the heart fill, then empty, with a message.

Hint: use optimistic updates for small, frequent, reversible actions, such as likes, ticks and reordering. For payments and anything hard to undo, show a clear “Saving…” state and wait for the server instead.

FavouriteButton.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { useMutation, useQuery, useQueryClient } from "@tanstack/vue-query";
import { getProduct, setFavourite, type Product } from "../api";

const props = defineProps<{ id: number }>();
const client = useQueryClient();
const key = ["product", props.id];
const product = useQuery({ queryKey: key, queryFn: () => getProduct(props.id) });

const mutation = useMutation({
  mutationFn: (favourite: boolean) => setFavourite(props.id, favourite),
  // 1. Before the server answers: change the cache, and remember the old value
  onMutate: async (favourite) => {
    await client.cancelQueries({ queryKey: key });
    const previous = client.getQueryData<Product>(key);
    client.setQueryData<Product>(key, (old) => (old ? { ...old, favourite } : old));
    return { previous };
  },
  // 2. If it fails: put the old value back
  onError: (_error, _favourite, context) => client.setQueryData(key, context?.previous),
  // 3. Either way: ask the server for the truth
  onSettled: () => client.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: key }),
});
</script>

<template>
  <p v-if="!product.data.value" class="muted">Loading…</p>
  <div v-else class="card">
    <h2>{{ product.data.value.name }}</h2>
    <button
      type="button"
      :aria-pressed="product.data.value.favourite"
      :class="{ secondary: !product.data.value.favourite }"
      @click="mutation.mutate(!product.data.value.favourite)"
    >
      {{ product.data.value.favourite ? "♥" : "♡" }} Favourite
    </button>
    <span v-if="mutation.isPending.value" class="muted"> Saving…</span>
    <p v-if="mutation.isError.value" class="error" role="alert">Could not save. The change was undone.</p>
  </div>
</template>

Exercises

  1. Easy Add an “About” page at /about with a RouterLink in the navigation bar.
  2. Easy On the details page, show “Product 2 of 4” using the cached list, if it is already in the cache, with useQueryClient().getQueryData(["products", null]).
  3. Medium Add a search box to the products page that keeps its text in the address as ?q=, together with the category, and filters the list by name.
  4. Medium Give every route a meta.title, and set document.title after each navigation with router.afterEach.
  5. Challenge Add a “Next product” link to the details page, and make it feel instant: when the page loads, call queryClient.prefetchQuery for the next product, so it is already cached when the user clicks.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 8

Users do not only read data; they send it, through forms. In the next chapter you will build forms that validate as the user types, show helpful errors, describe their rules once in a schema shared with TypeScript, and send the result to the server safely.

8

Chapter 8

Forms and Validation

In this chapter you will learn to

  • bind every kind of input with v-model and its modifiers
  • describe a form’s rules once, in a zod schema that also gives you the TypeScript type
  • build a small form composable: values, errors, touched fields and submitting
  • show errors at the right moment, accessibly, and move the focus to them
  • show errors that only the server can find, under the right field
  • split long forms into steps that check only their own fields

Forms are where users give your app their data: a name, an address, a password, money. They are also where most frustration on the web happens: rules that are not explained, errors that appear too early or too late, typed text that vanishes after a failed submit. A good form is mostly a list of small decisions made well. This chapter makes them once, in a schema and a composable that every form can reuse.

Bit

The browser checks nothing that matters on its own, and the server must check everything again anyway. So why validate in the form at all? Because it is the only place where you can help the user fix the mistake while they are still typing.

8.1 v-model on every kind of input

In Vue, the value of a field lives in your state, and v-model keeps the two in step. It adapts to the kind of input:

  • A text input or <textarea> binds a string. v-model.trim removes spaces at the ends, v-model.number converts to a number, and v-model.lazy updates only when the field is left.
  • A checkbox binds a boolean, or, with an array, the list of checked values.
  • Radio buttons with the same v-model bind the value of the chosen one.
  • A <select> binds the value of the chosen option.

Because the values are in state, everything else is ordinary Vue: a computed can check them, a watcher can save a draft, and the template can show anything derived from them.

8.2 One schema for the rules

Every form has rules: required fields, formats, limits. Written by hand, they end up in three places: the form, the TypeScript type, and the server. zod, which you met in Chapter 2, describes them once:

contact-schema.ts
import * as z from "zod";

// One schema: the rules, the error messages, and (with z.infer) the TypeScript type
export const contactSchema = z.object({
  name: z.string().trim().min(3, "Please write your full name (at least 3 letters)."),
  email: z.email("Please enter an email like name@example.com."),
  age: z.coerce.number<string>().int("Age must be a whole number.").min(13, "You must be 13 or older."),
  message: z.string().trim().min(20, "Please write at least 20 characters."),
});

export type Contact = z.infer<typeof contactSchema>;
  • Each field has its rules and its own message, written for the user: what is wrong and how to fix it.
  • z.coerce.number() turns the text of the age field into a number before checking it. Inputs always give text; the schema decides what it becomes.
  • z.infer gives the type of the result, with age as a number and the name trimmed, so the code after validation is fully typed.

The same schema runs anywhere, including on the server, where it must run again, because anyone can send data without your form:

validate.ts
import { contactSchema } from "./contact-schema";

// The same schema checks data that arrives at the server
const good = contactSchema.safeParse({
  name: "  Sara Khan ",
  email: "sara@example.com",
  age: "27",
  message: "I would like to order twenty copies for my class.",
});
console.log("Valid:", good.success, good.data);

const bad = contactSchema.safeParse({ name: "S", email: "sara@", age: "9", message: "Hi" });
console.log("Invalid:", bad.success);
for (const issue of bad.error?.issues ?? []) {
  console.log(`  ${issue.path.join(".")}: ${issue.message}`);
}
Terminal
Valid: true {
  name: 'Sara Khan',
  email: 'sara@example.com',
  age: 27,
  message: 'I would like to order twenty copies for my class.'
}
Invalid: false
  name: Please write your full name (at least 3 letters).
  email: Please enter an email like name@example.com.
  age: You must be 13 or older.
  message: Please write at least 20 characters.

8.3 A form composable

A real form needs more than a schema: it must know which fields the user has visited, whether a submit was tried, whether it is sending, and where to put errors that come back from the server. This is the same work for every form, so it belongs in a composable, as in Chapter 6:

useForm.ts
import { computed, nextTick, reactive, ref, watch } from "vue";
import type * as z from "zod";

type Errors<K extends PropertyKey> = Partial<Record<K, string>>;

// A small form composable: values, errors, touched fields and submitting, around one schema
export function useForm<S extends z.ZodObject>(schema: S, initial: z.input<S>) {
  type Key = keyof z.input<S> & string;
  const values = reactive({ ...initial }) as z.input<S>;
  const allErrors = ref<Errors<Key>>({});
  const touched = reactive(new Set<string>());
  const submitted = ref(false);
  const submitting = ref(false);

  function check(): z.output<S> | undefined {
    const result = schema.safeParse(values);
    const next: Errors<Key> = {};
    for (const issue of result.success ? [] : result.error.issues) next[issue.path[0] as Key] ??= issue.message;
    allErrors.value = next;
    return result.success ? result.data : undefined;
  }
  watch(values, check, { deep: true });

  // Show a field's error only after the user has left it once, or tried to submit
  const errors = computed(() => {
    const shown: Errors<Key> = {};
    for (const [key, message] of Object.entries(allErrors.value) as [Key, string][]) {
      if (submitted.value || touched.has(key)) shown[key] = message;
    }
    return shown;
  });

  async function focusFirstError() {
    await nextTick();
    document.querySelector<HTMLElement>("[aria-invalid='true']")?.focus();
  }

  /** Checks some fields only (one step of a longer form), and shows their errors. */
  function checkFields(fields: readonly Key[]) {
    fields.forEach((f) => touched.add(f));
    check();
    const ok = fields.every((f) => !allErrors.value[f]);
    if (!ok) void focusFirstError();
    return ok;
  }

  function handleSubmit(send: (data: z.output<S>) => Promise<Errors<Key> | void>) {
    return async () => {
      submitted.value = true;
      const data = check();
      if (!data) return focusFirstError();
      submitting.value = true;
      try {
        const fromServer = await send(data);
        // Errors only the server knows go under the right field, like any other
        if (fromServer) {
          allErrors.value = fromServer;
          void focusFirstError();
        }
      } finally {
        submitting.value = false;
      }
    };
  }

  return { values, errors, submitting, checkFields, handleSubmit, blur: (field: Key) => touched.add(field) };
}

The composable keeps the values in a reactive object, checks them with the schema whenever they change, and exposes only the errors the user should see now. handleSubmit wraps the code that sends the data: it marks the form as submitted, stops and moves the focus if anything is wrong, and passes the parsed data, typed and converted, to your function.

Each field also needs the same markup around it: a label, the input, and an error linked to the input. A component with a scoped slot writes it once:

FormField.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
defineProps<{ id: string; label: string; error?: string | undefined }>();
</script>

<template>
  <div class="field">
    <label :for="id">{{ label }}</label>
    <!-- The slot receives the attributes that link the input to its label and its error -->
    <slot :id="id" :aria-invalid="!!error" :aria-describedby="error ? `${id}-error` : undefined" />
    <p v-if="error" :id="`${id}-error`" class="error">{{ error }}</p>
  </div>
</template>

The slot hands its attributes to the input, so the label’s for, the input’s aria-invalid and aria-describedby, and the error’s id always match. A screen reader reading the field also reads its error. And the form itself becomes a short list of fields:

ContactForm.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { ref } from "vue";
import { contactSchema } from "./contact-schema";
import FormField from "./FormField.vue";
import { useForm } from "./useForm";

const { values, errors, submitting, blur, handleSubmit } = useForm(contactSchema, {
  name: "",
  email: "",
  age: "",
  message: "",
});
const sent = ref(false);

const submit = handleSubmit(async (contact) => {
  // contact.age is a number here, not text: the schema converted it
  await new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, 600)); // send it to the server
  sent.value = true;
});
</script>

<template>
  <p v-if="sent" class="card done" role="status">Thank you! We will reply within two days.</p>
  <form v-else novalidate @submit.prevent="submit">
    <FormField id="name" v-slot="attrs" label="Full name" :error="errors.name">
      <input v-bind="attrs" v-model="values.name" autocomplete="name" @blur="blur('name')" />
    </FormField>
    <FormField id="email" v-slot="attrs" label="Email" :error="errors.email">
      <input v-bind="attrs" v-model="values.email" type="email" autocomplete="email" dir="ltr" @blur="blur('email')" />
    </FormField>
    <FormField id="age" v-slot="attrs" label="Age" :error="errors.age">
      <input v-bind="attrs" v-model="values.age" inputmode="numeric" @blur="blur('age')" />
    </FormField>
    <FormField id="message" v-slot="attrs" label="Message" :error="errors.message">
      <textarea v-bind="attrs" v-model="values.message" rows="3" @blur="blur('message')" />
    </FormField>
    <button type="submit" :disabled="submitting">{{ submitting ? "Sending…" : "Send" }}</button>
  </form>
</template>

When to show errors

Errors shown while the user is still typing the first letters are noise: “invalid email” after one letter is not helpful, it is rude. Errors shown only after submit are too late. The composable follows the rule that most good forms use: a field’s error appears when the user leaves the field (@blur), or tries to submit. After that, it updates as the user types, so it disappears the moment the value is fixed.

Tip: help the browser help the user

The right type, inputmode and autocomplete cost nothing and save users a lot of typing: type="email" shows the @ key on phones, inputmode="numeric" shows digits, and autocomplete="email" lets the browser fill in a saved address. Emails, phone numbers and passwords also get dir="ltr", so they read correctly in Arabic pages.

Quick check

  1. Why must the server validate the data again, even if the form already did?
  2. What does z.infer give you?
  3. Why does the composable wait until a field is left before showing its error?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

8.4 Errors only the server knows

Some rules cannot be checked in the browser: is this email already registered? Is this coupon still valid? The server answers with errors per field, and they should appear exactly like the form’s own errors, under the field, with the focus moved there. In useForm, the function passed to handleSubmit can return such errors; Project 8.2 uses it for an email that already has an account.

Watch out: never lose what the user typed

After a failed submit, keep every value in the form. The only exceptions are passwords and card numbers, which many sites clear on purpose. Nothing makes users leave faster than retyping a long form because one field was wrong.

8.5 Forms in several steps

A long form, such as a checkout, is easier in steps. The data is still one object with one schema; each step lists the fields it is responsible for, and “Next” checks only those, with checkFields. The last step shows a summary and submits the whole form, checked against the whole schema. Project 8.3 builds one.

8.6 Form libraries

The composable in this chapter is about seventy lines, and it is enough for many apps. Large apps with many complex forms often use a library, which adds features such as field arrays, async rules and fine-grained performance:

  • VeeValidate is the most used in Vue. At the time of writing, its stable version 4.15 accepts zod 3 schemas through @vee-validate/zod, and version 5, still in beta, adds support for zod 4 and other schema libraries directly.
  • TanStack Form, from the authors of TanStack Query, accepts zod 4 schemas directly.

All of them do what you just built: values, errors, touched fields, submitting and server errors, around a schema. Knowing how it works inside lets you choose one with open eyes, or decide you do not need one.

Professional habit: write the error messages with the design

Every rule needs a message that says what is wrong and how to fix it, in the user’s language: not “Invalid input” but “Please enter an 11-digit Egyptian mobile number.” Write the messages when the form is designed, review them like any other text, and keep them next to the rules, in the schema.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Rules only in the browserAnyone can send bad data straight to the serverCheck the same schema on the server
Rules written in three placesThe form, the type and the server disagreeOne schema, with z.infer for the type
Errors shown on the first keystrokeA form that scolds the userShow them after the field is left, or after submit
Errors not linked to their inputScreen readers never read themaria-invalid and aria-describedby
Placeholders instead of labelsThe hint disappears when typing startsA real <label> for every field
Clearing the form after a server errorUsers retype everythingKeep the values; show the error under the field
Using the text of a number field as a number"27" + 1 is "271"Let the schema convert, with z.coerce

Summary

  • v-model binds every kind of input to state; .trim, .number and .lazy adjust it.
  • A zod schema holds the rules and the messages once, converts the values, and gives the type with z.infer. The server checks it again.
  • A form composable keeps values, errors, touched fields and the submitting state, and passes parsed data to your submit function.
  • Show errors after a field is left or a submit is tried, linked with aria-describedby, and move the focus to the first one.
  • Server errors go under the right field; never clear what the user typed.
  • Steps check only their own fields; the last step checks the whole schema.

Quick check answers: 1. Because anyone can send data to the server without the form, for example with a script, so the form’s checks protect nothing. 2. The TypeScript type of the data after validation, with conversions applied, such as age as a number. 3. Because errors on the first letters only scold the user for an answer they have not finished writing.

Practice projects

For each form, write the schema first, with every error message, and test it with safeParse in Node before you build any screen. When the rules are right, the form is mostly wiring.

Project 8.1 · Easy

An accessible contact form

Finish the contact form from this chapter, and make sure every field is labelled, linked to its error, and receives the focus when it is wrong.

You will practise: useForm, a field component with a scoped slot, @blur, and accessibility attributes.

  1. Write the schema and infer the type. Give every rule a message that explains the fix.
  2. Write FormField: a label, a scoped slot for the input, and the error paragraph with an id. Pass id, aria-invalid and aria-describedby through the slot.
  3. Bind each input with v-model and v-bind="attrs", and call blur("name") on @blur.
  4. Try every mistake: leave fields empty, type a short name, a broken email, an age of 9. Check that errors appear only after leaving a field, and disappear as soon as the value is fixed.
  5. Test with a screen reader (VoiceOver, NVDA or TalkBack): submit an empty form and listen. The focus should land on the first field, and its error should be read with its label.

Hint: the focus moves to the first [aria-invalid='true'] element after nextTick, because the attribute only appears once Vue has updated the page. Without the await nextTick(), there would be nothing to focus yet.

Project 8.2 · Medium

Sign up, with an answer from the server

A sign-up form with a password, its confirmation and a terms box, which also shows the server’s “email already used” message on the email field.

You will practise: rules that compare two fields with refine, z.literal(true) for a required checkbox, server errors returned from the submit function, and the submitting state.

  1. Write the schema: an email, a password of at least 8 characters with a digit, a confirmation, and terms: z.literal(true, "…").
  2. Compare the passwords with .refine(…, { path: ["confirm"] }), so the error appears under the confirmation field.
  3. In the submit function, check the full schema, then call a pretend server that answers { email: "…" } for one particular email. Return that object, and useForm shows it under the email field.
  4. Disable the button while submitting is true, so a double click cannot create two accounts.
  5. Give each password input autocomplete="new-password", so password managers offer to create a strong one.

Hint: design your API so that its errors name the field they belong to, as the pretend server does here. Then the frontend can put every server error in the right place with one line of code.

signup-schema.ts
import * as z from "zod";

export const signupSchema = z
  .object({
    email: z.email("Please enter an email like name@example.com."),
    password: z.string().min(8, "At least 8 characters, with a number.").regex(/\d/, "At least 8 characters, with a number."),
    confirm: z.string(),
    terms: z.literal(true, "Please accept the terms to continue."),
  })
  .refine((data) => data.password === data.confirm, { message: "The passwords do not match.", path: ["confirm"] });

Project 8.3 · Challenge

A checkout in three steps

Delivery, payment and review as three steps of one form, checked step by step, with a review screen before the order is placed.

You will practise: z.enum, checking some fields only, <select> and radio buttons with v-model, aria-current="step", and keyed buttons.

  1. Write one schema for the whole order, and a STEPS list with each step’s title and fields.
  2. Show only the current step’s inputs. “Next” calls checkFields(STEPS[step].fields) and moves on only if it returns true.
  3. On the last step, show a summary from values and a “Place order” submit button.
  4. Show the progress as a list, with aria-current="step" on the current one.
  5. Give “Next” and “Place order” different keys. Without them, Vue may reuse the same <button> and only change its type, and a click on “Next” could end as a submit of the new type, placing the order one step early.

Hint: because all steps are one form, “Back” is free: the values are still there. Never create a separate form per step unless the steps are saved separately on the server.

checkout-schema.ts
import * as z from "zod";

export const checkoutSchema = z.object({
  city: z.enum(["cairo", "alexandria", "giza"], "Please choose a city."),
  street: z.string().trim().min(5, "Please write the street and building number."),
  phone: z.string().regex(/^01[0125]\d{8}$/, "Please enter an 11-digit Egyptian mobile number."),
  method: z.enum(["card", "cash"]),
});

// Each step lists the fields it is responsible for
export const STEPS = [
  { title: "Delivery", fields: ["city", "street", "phone"] },
  { title: "Payment", fields: ["method"] },
  { title: "Review", fields: [] },
] as const;

Exercises

  1. Easy Add an optional “Phone” field to the contact form, which is checked only when something is typed in it.
  2. Easy Show a counter under the message, “12 of at least 20 characters”, computed from values.message.
  3. Medium Add a dirty computed to useForm that is true when any value differs from the initial values, and ask for confirmation before leaving the page while it is true, with the beforeunload event.
  4. Medium Save a draft of the contact form in localStorage while the user types, and restore it when the form opens.
  5. Challenge Make the email field of the sign-up form check, after the user leaves it, whether the email is already taken, with a debounced request, and show the answer under the field before the user even submits.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 9

Some data is needed by many distant parts of the app: who is signed in, the chosen language, the cart. The next chapter gives it a home with Pinia, Vue’s official store, and shows how to keep that home small.

9

Chapter 9

Global State with Pinia

In this chapter you will learn to

  • sort every piece of state into the right home: component, address, server cache or global store
  • share values with distant components through provide and inject, with typed keys
  • build a global store with Pinia, and read it without losing reactivity
  • save a store between visits
  • use a store outside components, watch its actions, and test it without any screen

Some data is needed everywhere: who is signed in, the chosen language and theme, the number of items in the cart. Passing it as props from the top of the app through every component in between is called prop drilling, and it makes every component in the path depend on data it never uses. Vue offers two answers: provide and inject, built into Vue, and Pinia, Vue’s official store. But first, the most important skill of this chapter: knowing that most state is not global.

Bit

Many apps have a giant global store full of data that one screen uses, or that really belongs to the server. Every time I am tempted to make something global, I first ask: who actually reads this?

9.1 Four homes for state

Kind of stateExamplesIts home
Belongs to one part of the screenAn open menu, a form’s values, the current tabA ref in the lowest common component (Chapter 5)
Should survive a reload or be shareableFilters, search text, page numberThe address (Chapter 7)
A copy of server dataProducts, orders, the user’s profileThe query cache (Chapter 7)
Owned by the app, read in many placesSigned-in user, theme, language, cart, notificationsprovide/inject or a Pinia store (this chapter)

Go down the table in order and stop at the first home that fits. In a typical app, only a handful of values reach the last row.

9.2 provide and inject

A component can provide a value to everything below it in the tree, at any depth, and any of those components can inject it, without props in between. Wrapped in two small functions, it looks like this:

auth.ts
import { inject, provide, readonly, ref, type InjectionKey, type Ref } from "vue";

type User = { name: string };
type Auth = { user: Readonly<Ref<User | null>>; signIn: (name: string) => void; signOut: () => void };

// A typed key: inject() knows the type of what was provided
const AuthKey: InjectionKey<Auth> = Symbol("auth");

export function provideAuth() {
  const user = ref<User | null>(null);
  provide(AuthKey, {
    user: readonly(user),
    signIn: (name) => (user.value = { name }),
    signOut: () => (user.value = null),
  });
}

// A composable hides inject(), and fails clearly if the provider is missing
export function useAuth(): Auth {
  const auth = inject(AuthKey);
  if (!auth) throw new Error("useAuth() must be used inside a component that called provideAuth()");
  return auth;
}
AuthApp.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { provideAuth } from "./auth";
import Profile from "./Profile.vue";
import UserMenu from "./UserMenu.vue";

provideAuth();
</script>

<template>
  <header>
    <span class="brand">Bit's shop</span>
    <UserMenu />
  </header>
  <main>
    <Profile />
  </main>
</template>
UserMenu.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { useAuth } from "./auth";

// Any component under the provider can read the user, however deep
const { user, signIn, signOut } = useAuth();
</script>

<template>
  <button v-if="!user" type="button" @click="signIn('Sara')">Sign in</button>
  <span v-else class="row">
    Hello, {{ user.name }}
    <button type="button" class="secondary" @click="signOut">Sign out</button>
  </span>
</template>

Three habits make provide and inject pleasant to use:

  • Use a typed InjectionKey, a Symbol that carries the type. inject(AuthKey) then knows exactly what it returns, and two libraries can never clash over a name.
  • Hide the key behind composables. Components call provideAuth() and useAuth(), never inject directly, and useAuth throws a clear error if someone forgets the provider, instead of a mysterious undefined later.
  • Provide data read-only, with the functions that change it. readonly(user) means components cannot assign to it; the only way to change the user is signIn and signOut, so it is always clear who changed what.

provide works best for values that belong to a part of the tree: a theme for one area, a form’s context for its fields, the settings of a library component. For state that the whole app shares, a store is clearer.

Quick check

  1. Where should the text of a search box live if users want to share the results?
  2. What happens when a component calls useAuth() without a provider above it?
  3. Why is the user provided as readonly?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

9.3 A store with Pinia

A store holds state that lives outside any one component, together with the values derived from it and the functions that change it. In Pinia, a store is defined with defineStore and, in the setup store style, written exactly like a composable: refs are the state, computed values are the getters, and functions are the actions:

stores/cart.ts
import { computed } from "vue";
import { defineStore } from "pinia";
import { useLocalStorage } from "@vueuse/core";

export type Product = { id: number; name: string; price: number };
type Line = { product: Product; quantity: number };

// A setup store: refs are state, computed are getters, functions are actions
export const useCart = defineStore("cart", () => {
  // useLocalStorage keeps the lines in the browser and loads them on the next visit
  const lines = useLocalStorage<Record<number, Line>>("cart", {});

  const count = computed(() => Object.values(lines.value).reduce((n, l) => n + l.quantity, 0));
  const total = computed(() => Object.values(lines.value).reduce((n, l) => n + l.quantity * l.product.price, 0));

  function add(product: Product) {
    const line = lines.value[product.id];
    if (line) line.quantity++;
    else lines.value[product.id] = { product, quantity: 1 };
  }

  function clear() {
    lines.value = {};
  }

  return { lines, count, total, add, clear };
});

The difference from a composable with module-level refs (Chapter 6) is what Pinia adds around it: one instance per app, which matters for server rendering and tests; the Vue devtools, which show every store, its state and every change; and the hooks you will meet in section 9.5. Install it once, in main.ts:

main.ts
import { createApp } from "vue";
import { createPinia } from "pinia";
import ShopApp from "./ShopApp.vue";

createApp(ShopApp).use(createPinia()).mount("#app");

Then any component can use the store. Here three of them share one cart, with no props and no provider between them:

CartBadge.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { storeToRefs } from "pinia";
import { useCart } from "./stores/cart";

// storeToRefs keeps state and getters reactive after destructuring; actions are plain functions
const { count } = storeToRefs(useCart());
</script>

<template>
  <span class="row">Cart <span class="badge">{{ count }}</span></span>
</template>
ProductList.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { useCart, type Product } from "./stores/cart";

const products: Product[] = [
  { id: 1, name: "Code from Zero", price: 25 },
  { id: 2, name: "Bit mug", price: 8 },
  { id: 3, name: "Bit cap", price: 12 },
];
const { add } = useCart();
</script>

<template>
  <section class="card">
    <div v-for="p in products" :key="p.id" class="row spread">
      <span>{{ p.name }}</span>
      <button type="button" @click="add(p)">Add</button>
    </div>
  </section>
</template>
CartSummary.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { storeToRefs } from "pinia";
import { useCart } from "./stores/cart";

const cart = useCart();
const { lines, total } = storeToRefs(cart);
</script>

<template>
  <p v-if="Object.keys(lines).length === 0" class="card muted">The cart is empty.</p>
  <section v-else class="card">
    <ul>
      <li v-for="l in lines" :key="l.product.id">
        <span>{{ l.quantity }} × {{ l.product.name }}</span>
        <span>{{ l.quantity * l.product.price }} EGP</span>
      </li>
    </ul>
    <p><b>Total: {{ total }} EGP</b></p>
    <button type="button" class="secondary" @click="cart.clear()">Empty the cart</button>
  </section>
</template>

Watch out: destructuring a store

A store is a reactive object, so const { count } = useCart() copies the current number and never updates, exactly the trap of Chapter 5. Use storeToRefs(store) for state and getters, which gives refs linked to the store. Actions are plain functions and can be destructured directly, as ProductList does with add.

9.4 Saving a store between visits

The cart above keeps its lines in useLocalStorage from VueUse (Chapter 6), so they are saved in the browser on every change and loaded on the next visit, and even kept in step between open tabs. Because a setup store is just a function, any composable works inside it. Larger apps sometimes use a Pinia plugin, such as pinia-plugin-persistedstate, to save several stores the same way with one line each.

Tip: save only what you must

Save the cart, the theme, a draft. Never save server data, which goes stale, nor anything secret: localStorage is readable by any script on the page (Chapter 14). And if you change the shape of saved data later, old visitors still have the old shape, so read it carefully.

9.5 Stores outside components

A store does not need a screen. With an active Pinia, it runs anywhere, which is how you test it, and how a router guard or a plain module reads it. Pinia also lets you watch a store from outside: $onAction sees every action call, and $subscribe sees every change of state:

store-node.ts
import { computed, ref } from "vue";
import { createPinia, defineStore, setActivePinia } from "pinia";

// A store works without any component: the same code runs in Node and in tests
const useCounter = defineStore("counter", () => {
  const count = ref(0);
  const double = computed(() => count.value * 2);
  const increase = (by = 1) => (count.value += by);
  return { count, double, increase };
});

setActivePinia(createPinia());
const counter = useCounter();

counter.$onAction(({ name, args }) => console.log("action:", name, "with", args));
counter.$subscribe(() => console.log("changed:", counter.count, "→ double", counter.double), { flush: "sync" });

counter.increase();
counter.increase(5);
counter.$dispose();
counter.increase();
console.log("after $dispose:", counter.count);
Terminal
action: increase with []
changed: 1 → double 2
action: increase with [ 5 ]
changed: 6 → double 12
after $dispose: 7

That is the same mechanism the devtools use to show a history of changes, and the one a logging or analytics plugin would use: one place that sees every change, without touching the components. After $dispose(), the hooks are gone: the last call changed the count but printed nothing.

Professional habit: keep server data out of global stores

Copying products or orders into a store means writing your own cache, by hand, without refetching, deduplication or invalidation. Let TanStack Query own server data, and keep stores for what the app itself owns. Most “global state” problems disappear with this one rule.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Making everything globalHard-to-follow changes from anywhereUse the table in section 9.1, top to bottom
inject("auth") with a stringNo types, and names that can clashA typed InjectionKey, behind a composable
Providing a writable refAny component can change shared stateProvide readonly state and functions that change it
Destructuring a storeValues that never updatestoreToRefs for state and getters
Calling useCart() at the top of a moduleAn error: no active Pinia yetCall it inside components, composables or functions
Server data copied into a storeStale data and hand-made caching bugsTanStack Query

Summary

  • State has four homes: the component, the address, the query cache and, for the few values the app owns and many places read, provide/inject or a store.
  • provide and inject pass values down any depth. Use a typed InjectionKey, composables around it, and readonly state.
  • A Pinia setup store is written like a composable: refs are state, computed are getters, functions are actions.
  • Read stores with storeToRefs; destructure actions directly.
  • Composables such as useLocalStorage work inside stores, for example to save them.
  • Stores run without components; $onAction and $subscribe watch them from outside, and tests use them directly.

Quick check answers: 1. In the address, as a query parameter. 2. It throws the error “useAuth() must be used inside a component that called provideAuth()”, which points straight at the problem. 3. So that no component can assign a new user by mistake; the only way to change it is through signIn and signOut.

Practice projects

Before each project, fill in the table from section 9.1 for the state you plan to keep. Everything that does not land in the last row stays out of the global code.

Project 9.1 · Easy

A theme that follows the user

A light and a dark theme, starting from the system setting, switchable from anywhere, and remembered on the next visit.

You will practise: provide and inject with a typed key, VueUse’s usePreferredDark and useLocalStorage, a computed choice, and watchEffect.

  1. Write the colours as CSS custom properties on :root, and the dark ones on html.dark. Components never mention colours by name.
  2. In provideTheme, keep the user’s saved choice, or null if they never chose, and fall back to the system setting with usePreferredDark().
  3. In a watchEffect, toggle the dark class on <html>.
  4. Provide { dark, toggle } with a typed key, and read it with useTheme(), which throws a clear error outside the provider.
  5. Make the switch a button with aria-pressed.

Hint: saving null until the user chooses means the app keeps following the system setting, and starts following the user only once they express a wish. That small detail is what users notice when they switch their phone to dark mode at night.

theme.ts
import { computed, inject, provide, watchEffect, type InjectionKey, type Ref } from "vue";
import { useLocalStorage, usePreferredDark } from "@vueuse/core";

type Theme = { dark: Readonly<Ref<boolean>>; toggle: () => void };
const ThemeKey: InjectionKey<Theme> = Symbol("theme");

export function provideTheme() {
  const system = usePreferredDark();
  const saved = useLocalStorage<boolean | null>("dark", null);
  const dark = computed(() => saved.value ?? system.value);
  watchEffect(() => document.documentElement.classList.toggle("dark", dark.value));
  provide(ThemeKey, { dark, toggle: () => (saved.value = !dark.value) });
}

export function useTheme(): Theme {
  const theme = inject(ThemeKey);
  if (!theme) throw new Error("useTheme() must be used inside a component that called provideTheme()");
  return theme;
}

Project 9.2 · Medium

A cart that survives a reload

Build the cart store of this chapter yourself, with add, decrease, remove and clear, a badge in the header, and saving between visits.

You will practise: a setup store, getters, actions that change state in place, storeToRefs, and testing a store in Node.

  1. Write the store with the four actions. Keep lines in an object keyed by product id, so finding a line needs no search.
  2. Make count and total getters with computed.
  3. Build three components: CartBadge (count only), ProductList (add only) and CartSummary (lines, total and the other actions).
  4. Keep the lines in useLocalStorage, add a few products, reload the page and check that they are still there.
  5. Write a Node script that creates a Pinia, runs the four actions, and prints the count after each.

Hint: if you change the shape of saved data later, old visitors will load the old shape. Save a version number with the lines, and convert or discard old data when you load it.

Project 9.3 · Challenge

Notifications with undo

A notification system that any component can use with one call, that shows at most three messages, disappears by itself, can offer an “Undo” button, and is announced by screen readers.

You will practise: a store used from many components, Teleport, timers, and live regions.

  1. Write a useToasts store with the list of messages and two actions, notify(text, action?) and dismiss(id).
  2. Add new messages with [...toasts.slice(-2), next], so at most three stay on screen.
  3. Remove each message after five seconds with setTimeout, and also with a close button labelled for screen readers.
  4. Render the list with <Teleport to="body">, so it is never hidden or clipped by the component that placed it.
  5. Give the list role="status" and aria-live="polite", so screen readers announce new messages without interrupting the user.

Hint: “Undo” is friendlier than “Are you sure?”. Do the action at once, and offer to reverse it for a few seconds. Users make fewer mistakes, and the rest of the time they are not slowed down.

toasts.ts
import { defineStore } from "pinia";
import { ref } from "vue";

type Action = { label: string; run: () => void };
type Toast = { id: number; text: string; action?: Action | undefined };

export const useToasts = defineStore("toasts", () => {
  const toasts = ref<Toast[]>([]);
  let next = 1;

  function dismiss(id: number) {
    toasts.value = toasts.value.filter((t) => t.id !== id);
  }

  function notify(text: string, action?: Action) {
    const id = next++;
    toasts.value = [...toasts.value.slice(-2), { id, text, action }];
    setTimeout(() => dismiss(id), 5000);
  }

  return { toasts, notify, dismiss };
});

Exercises

  1. Easy Add a remove(id) action to the cart store and a “×” button next to each line in CartSummary.
  2. Easy Write a useLanguage store that holds "en" or "ar", and sets lang and dir on document.documentElement in a watchEffect.
  3. Medium Write a Node script, like store-node.ts, that tests the cart’s add and clear, and prints count after each step. What does useLocalStorage do in Node, where there is no localStorage?
  4. Medium Use $subscribe to send every change of the cart to the console with the time, only in development (import.meta.env.DEV).
  5. Challenge Make the sign-in in auth.ts real: signIn calls a pretend API that takes 500 ms, the state becomes a discriminated union ("signed-out" | "signing-in" | "signed-in"), and the menu shows the right thing for each status. Then move it into a Pinia store and compare the two versions.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 10

You can now build any screen. The next part of the book is about building them well, as a team. It starts with design systems: shared components, tokens and documentation that make a hundred screens look and behave like one product.

10

Chapter 10

Design Systems and Component Libraries

In this chapter you will learn to

  • explain what a design system is, and why teams build one
  • keep every visual decision in design tokens, and generate CSS from them
  • separate primitive tokens from semantic tokens, so themes are easy
  • design component APIs that are small, predictable and hard to misuse
  • support both controlled and uncontrolled use of a component
  • document, test and version a component library

Open any big app made without a design system and count the shades of blue, the sizes of buttons, and the ways an error is shown. There will be dozens, because every screen was built by a different person on a different day. A design system is the shared set of decisions, tokens, components, patterns and their documentation, that makes a hundred screens look and behave like one product. For the team it means less code, fewer arguments and fewer accessibility bugs, because each decision is made, and tested, once.

Bit

Designers and developers often build two systems: one in the design tool and one in the code, slowly drifting apart. The best teams share one source of truth: the tokens. Book 9 shows the designer’s side in Figma; this chapter shows the developer’s.

10.1 Design tokens

A design token is a named design decision: a colour, a spacing step, a radius, a font size. Instead of writing #2b2b2b in fifty places, everyone writes var(--color-action). Tokens are kept in a neutral format, usually JSON, so that the same values can be turned into CSS for the web, into Swift and Kotlin for mobile apps, and into variables in the design tool.

tokens.json
{
  "primitive": {
    "indigo-50": "#eef2ff", "indigo-600": "#2b2b2b", "indigo-800": "#1a1a1a",
    "pink-600": "#2b2b2b", "red-50": "#fef2f2", "red-700": "#b91c1c",
    "green-50": "#ecfdf5", "green-700": "#047857", "amber-50": "#fffbeb", "amber-800": "#92400e",
    "gray-50": "#f5f5fb", "gray-400": "#a5a3bd", "gray-600": "#5b5873", "indigo-300": "#a5b4fc", "gray-900": "#1e1b2e", "gray-950": "#262626", "white": "#ffffff"
  },
  "semantic": {
    "light": {
      "surface": "white", "surface-muted": "gray-50", "text": "gray-900", "text-muted": "gray-600",
      "action": "indigo-600", "action-text": "white", "action-soft": "indigo-50", "action-soft-text": "indigo-800",
      "danger": "red-700", "danger-soft": "red-50", "success": "green-700", "success-soft": "green-50",
      "warning": "amber-800", "warning-soft": "amber-50", "focus": "pink-600"
    },
    "dark": {
      "surface": "gray-950", "surface-muted": "gray-900", "text": "gray-50", "text-muted": "gray-400",
      "action": "indigo-300", "action-text": "indigo-800", "action-soft": "indigo-800", "action-soft-text": "indigo-50",
      "danger": "red-50", "danger-soft": "red-700", "success": "green-50", "success-soft": "green-700",
      "warning": "amber-50", "warning-soft": "amber-800", "focus": "pink-600"
    }
  },
  "space": { "1": "4px", "2": "8px", "3": "12px", "4": "16px", "6": "24px" },
  "radius": { "sm": "6px", "md": "10px", "full": "999px" }
}

Notice the two levels:

  • Primitive tokens are the raw palette: indigo-600, red-700. They describe what a value is.
  • Semantic tokens describe what a value is for: action, danger, text-muted. Components only ever use semantic tokens.

That separation is what makes themes cheap. The dark theme does not touch a single component; it simply points action and surface at different primitives. The same idea gives you a high-contrast theme, or a second brand, for free.

A small script turns the JSON into CSS custom properties. In a real project it runs before every build, so the CSS can never disagree with the tokens:

build-tokens.ts
import { readFileSync } from "node:fs";

// Reads tokens.json and writes CSS variables for the light and dark themes
type Tokens = {
  primitive: Record<string, string>;
  semantic: Record<"light" | "dark", Record<string, string>>;
  space: Record<string, string>;
  radius: Record<string, string>;
};

const tokens = JSON.parse(readFileSync(new URL("./tokens.json", import.meta.url), "utf8")) as Tokens;

function resolve(name: string): string {
  const value = tokens.primitive[name];
  if (!value) throw new Error(`Unknown primitive token: ${name}`);
  return value;
}

function block(selector: string, vars: Record<string, string>): string {
  const lines = Object.entries(vars).map(([k, v]) => `  --${k}: ${v};`);
  return `${selector} {\n${lines.join("\n")}\n}`;
}

const light = Object.fromEntries(Object.entries(tokens.semantic.light).map(([k, v]) => [`color-${k}`, resolve(v)]));
const dark = Object.fromEntries(Object.entries(tokens.semantic.dark).map(([k, v]) => [`color-${k}`, resolve(v)]));
const space = Object.fromEntries(Object.entries(tokens.space).map(([k, v]) => [`space-${k}`, v]));
const radius = Object.fromEntries(Object.entries(tokens.radius).map(([k, v]) => [`radius-${k}`, v]));

const css = [block(":root", { ...light, ...space, ...radius }), block('[data-theme="dark"]', dark)].join("\n\n");
console.log(css.split("\n").slice(0, 8).join("\n"));
console.log("  …");
console.log(`(${Object.keys(light).length} colours × 2 themes, ${Object.keys(space).length} spaces, ${Object.keys(radius).length} radii)`);
Terminal
:root {
  --color-surface: #ffffff;
  --color-surface-muted: #f5f5fb;
  --color-text: #1e1b2e;
  --color-text-muted: #5b5873;
  --color-action: #2b2b2b;
  --color-action-text: #ffffff;
  --color-action-soft: #eef2ff;
  …
(15 colours × 2 themes, 5 spaces, 3 radii)

The script throws an error if a semantic token points at a primitive that does not exist. That one check catches typos that would otherwise show up as a missing colour on one screen, weeks later.

10.2 Designing a component’s API

The API of a component is its props, events and slots: everything other developers see. A good API is small, says what it means, and makes wrong use difficult. Here is a button built from the tokens:

components.css (part)
.btn { display: inline-flex; align-items: center; gap: var(--space-2); border: 2px solid transparent;
  border-radius: var(--radius-full); font: inherit; font-weight: 600; cursor: pointer; }
.btn:focus-visible { outline: 3px solid var(--color-focus); outline-offset: 2px; }
.btn:disabled { opacity: 0.55; cursor: not-allowed; }
.btn-sm { padding: 2px var(--space-3); font-size: 0.875rem; }
.btn-md { padding: var(--space-1) var(--space-4); }
.btn-lg { padding: var(--space-2) var(--space-6); font-size: 1.125rem; }
.btn-primary { background: var(--color-action); color: var(--color-action-text); }
.btn-secondary { background: var(--color-action-soft); color: var(--color-action-soft-text); }
.btn-danger { background: var(--color-danger); color: var(--color-surface); }
.btn-ghost { background: transparent; color: var(--color-action); border-color: var(--color-action-soft); }
.spinner { width: 1em; height: 1em; border: 2px solid currentColor; border-right-color: transparent; border-radius: 50%; animation: spin 0.8s linear infinite; }
@keyframes spin { to { transform: rotate(360deg); } }
@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) { .spinner { animation-duration: 3s; } }
.badge { display: inline-block; padding: 0 var(--space-2); border-radius: var(--radius-full); font-size: 0.8rem; font-weight: 700; }
.badge-neutral { background: var(--color-action-soft); color: var(--color-action-soft-text); }
.badge-success { background: var(--color-success-soft); color: var(--color-success); }
.badge-warning { background: var(--color-warning-soft); color: var(--color-warning); }
.badge-danger { background: var(--color-danger-soft); color: var(--color-danger); }
.alert { display: flex; gap: var(--space-2); padding: var(--space-3) var(--space-4); border-radius: var(--radius-md); border-inline-start: 4px solid; margin-bottom: var(--space-2); }
.alert-info { background: var(--color-action-soft); color: var(--color-action-soft-text); }
.alert-success { background: var(--color-success-soft); color: var(--color-success); }
.alert-warning { background: var(--color-warning-soft); color: var(--color-warning); }
.alert-danger { background: var(--color-danger-soft); color: var(--color-danger); }
.switch { display: inline-flex; align-items: center; gap: var(--space-3); background: none; border: 0; padding: var(--space-1) 0; font: inherit; color: inherit; cursor: pointer; }
.switch .track { width: 44px; height: 24px; border-radius: var(--radius-full); background: var(--color-surface); border: 2px solid var(--color-text-muted); position: relative; transition: background 0.2s; }
.switch .thumb { position: absolute; top: 2px; inset-inline-start: 2px; width: 16px; height: 16px; border-radius: 50%; background: var(--color-text-muted); transition: inset-inline-start 0.2s; }
.switch[aria-checked="true"] .track { background: var(--color-action); border-color: var(--color-action); }
.switch[aria-checked="true"] .thumb { inset-inline-start: 22px; background: var(--color-action-text); }
.switch:focus-visible .track { outline: 3px solid var(--color-focus); outline-offset: 2px; }
.gallery { display: flex; flex-direction: column; gap: var(--space-3); background: var(--color-surface); color: var(--color-text); padding: var(--space-4); border-radius: var(--radius-md); }
.row { display: flex; gap: var(--space-2); flex-wrap: wrap; align-items: center; }
Button.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
type Variant = "primary" | "secondary" | "danger" | "ghost";
type Size = "sm" | "md" | "lg";

const { variant = "primary", size = "md", loading = false, type = "button" } = defineProps<{
  variant?: Variant;
  size?: Size;
  loading?: boolean;
  disabled?: boolean;
  type?: "button" | "submit" | "reset";
}>();
</script>

<!-- Every native button prop (type, disabled, onClick, aria-…) still works -->
<template>
  <button :type="type" :class="['btn', `btn-${variant}`, `btn-${size}`]" :disabled="disabled || loading" :aria-busy="loading || undefined">
    <span v-if="loading" class="spinner" aria-hidden="true" />
    <slot />
  </button>
</template>
  • Variants as one prop with a union type, variant="danger", not separate booleans like primary, danger, ghost. With booleans, nothing stops someone from writing <Button primary danger>, and nobody knows what that means.
  • Build on the platform. Vue passes every attribute the component does not declare as a prop, such as aria-label, form or a @click listener, straight to the root <button>. This attribute fallthrough means the design system does not have to list them one by one.
  • Safe defaults. type="button" by default, because a plain <button> inside a form submits it, a classic surprise. A loading button is also disabled, and tells assistive technology with aria-busy.
  • An escape hatch. A class passed by the parent is merged with the component’s own classes, not replaced, for the rare screen that needs a margin.

Tip: name things by purpose, never by appearance

A variant called blue becomes a lie the day the brand turns green. primary, danger and subtle stay true. The same rule applies to tokens: color-action, not color-indigo.

10.3 Composition over configuration

Components in a design system tend to grow props until nobody can use them: showIcon, iconPosition, titleSize, footerAlign… Each prop answers one screen’s wish, and together they form a maze. The cure is composition: instead of describing the content with props, let the user pass the content in. A card that accepts children can hold any header, picture or footer, today and next year, without a single new prop.

A good rule: props for how it behaves and looks (variant, size, disabled), children or slots for what it contains.

Controlled and uncontrolled

Inputs in a design system should work the way native inputs do: uncontrolled when the user just wants a default and does not care about every change, and controlled when the page owns the value. In Vue, both cases are covered by one line, defineModel: when the parent binds v-model the parent owns the value, and when it does not, the component keeps its own. Project 10.2 builds a switch that works both ways.

Quick check

  1. Should a button component use the token indigo-600 or color-action?
  2. What is wrong with props like primary, secondary and danger as three booleans?
  3. Why should a button component default to type="button"?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

10.4 Accessibility, built in once

The greatest gift of a design system is that accessibility is solved once, by people who took the time, instead of fifty times by people in a hurry. Every component in the system should come with: the right element or ARIA role, a visible focus style from the focus token, full keyboard support, colours that pass contrast in every theme, and a reduced-motion version of any animation.

Complex widgets, such as menus, dialogs, comboboxes and date pickers, are hard to get right. Many teams build them on headless libraries, which provide the behaviour and accessibility but no styles, and add their own tokens on top. For Vue, popular choices are Reka UI and Headless UI.

10.5 Documenting, testing and releasing

A component nobody can find is a component somebody will write again. Design systems are documented in a living catalogue, usually built with Storybook, where each component appears in all its variants and states, with its props, its do’s and don’ts, and the code to copy. Each of those examples, called a story, doubles as a test case:

  • Unit and interaction tests check behaviour: does the switch toggle with Space, does the loading button refuse clicks? (Chapter 12.)
  • Accessibility checks such as axe run on every story automatically. (Chapter 11.)
  • Visual regression tests take a screenshot of every story and compare it with the approved one, so a change to a shared token cannot silently break a screen.

A design system used by several apps is published as a package, with semantic versions and a changelog (Chapter 3): renaming a prop is a breaking change and needs a major version, so app teams can upgrade when they are ready.

Professional habit: start small, extract from real screens

Do not design forty components before the first screen exists. Build the product, notice what repeats, and promote it into the system when it has been used three times. A small system that everyone uses beats a large one that nobody trusts.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Hard-coded colours and sizes in componentsThemes and rebrands mean editing every fileSemantic tokens only
Tokens named after their look (blue)The names lie after a redesignName by purpose (action)
A boolean prop per variantImpossible combinationsOne variant prop with a union type
Copying native props one by oneMissing props such as form or aria-*Extend the native element’s props and pass the rest on
A prop for every layout wishA component nobody understandsComposition with children or slots
Checking a theme only in light modeStates that look the same, or text that vanishes, in dark modeShow every story in every theme

Summary

  • A design system is shared tokens, components, patterns and documentation that make a product consistent and accessible.
  • Design tokens are named decisions kept in a neutral format and generated into CSS and other platforms.
  • Primitive tokens are the palette; semantic tokens say what a value is for. Components use semantic tokens, and themes remap them.
  • Good component APIs use one variant prop, extend native props, have safe defaults, and prefer composition to configuration.
  • Inputs support controlled and uncontrolled use, like native inputs.
  • Accessibility is built into each component once. Stories document the system and drive unit, accessibility and visual tests; releases follow semantic versioning.

Quick check answers: 1. color-action: a semantic token, so themes and rebrands work without touching the component. 2. Nothing stops two of them being true together, and nobody knows what that should look like; one variant prop with a union type allows exactly one. 3. Because a plain <button> inside a form is a submit button, and would submit the form by accident.

Practice projects

Build these as the first pieces of your own small design system: one folder of tokens, one stylesheet generated from them, and a few components that use nothing else.

Project 10.1 · Easy

Badges and alerts from tokens

Two small components that show status: a badge for short labels and an alert for messages, each with four tones and no colour of its own.

You will practise: tone props with union types, semantic tokens, and choosing the right live-region role.

  1. Add soft background tokens for each tone (success-soft, warning-soft, danger-soft) and the matching text tokens, in both themes.
  2. Write the CSS classes .badge-… and .alert-… using only those tokens.
  3. Write Badge with a tone prop and children.
  4. Write Alert: an icon hidden from screen readers, the message, and a role: alert for errors, which interrupts, and status for the others, which waits for a pause.
  5. Never let colour carry the meaning alone: the words “Paid” and “Pending”, and the icons, say it too.

Hint: using role="alert" for everything sounds safe, but it interrupts the user for every small notice. Keep interruptions for things that really need attention now.

Alert.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
type Tone = "info" | "success" | "warning" | "danger";
const ICONS: Record<Tone, string> = { info: "ℹ", success: "✓", warning: "!", danger: "✕" };

const { tone = "info" } = defineProps<{ tone?: Tone }>();
</script>

<template>
  <!-- Errors interrupt (alert); everything else waits for a pause (status) -->
  <div :class="['alert', `alert-${tone}`]" :role="tone === 'danger' ? 'alert' : 'status'">
    <span aria-hidden="true">{{ ICONS[tone] }}</span>
    <span><slot /></span>
  </div>
</template>

Project 10.2 · Medium

A switch that works both ways

An on/off switch that can be used controlled or uncontrolled, works with the keyboard and screen readers, and is clear in every theme.

You will practise: the controlled/uncontrolled pattern, role="switch" with aria-checked, logical CSS properties, and checking states in every theme.

  1. Make the value a model with defineModel<boolean>({ default: false }). When the parent binds v-model, the parent owns the value; when it does not, the ref is local and the switch keeps its own.
  2. Render a real <button type="button"> with role="switch" and aria-checked. Enter and Space work for free.
  3. Draw the track and thumb with tokens, and move the thumb with inset-inline-start, so it slides the right way in Arabic too.
  4. Give the off state an outline and the on state a fill. Check both in the dark theme: when this book was written, the first version looked the same on and off in dark mode, until the screenshot showed it.
  5. Use it both ways: two switches without v-model, which remember their own state, and one with v-model, where it switches the theme of its own panel.

Hint: decide once, in the parent, whether it owns the value. Adding or removing v-model on the same switch while it is on screen makes it jump between two values: its own, and the parent’s.

Switch.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
// With v-model the parent owns the value; without it, defineModel keeps a local one
const on = defineModel<boolean>({ default: false });
defineProps<{ label: string }>();
</script>

<template>
  <button type="button" role="switch" :aria-checked="on" class="switch" @click="on = !on">
    <span class="track" aria-hidden="true"><span class="thumb" /></span>
    {{ label }}
  </button>
</template>

Project 10.3 · Challenge

A contrast guard for the tokens

A script that checks every important text-and-background pair in every theme against WCAG, and fails the build when one is too weak.

You will practise: reading JSON in Node, the contrast formula, typed tuples, and using the exit code to stop a pipeline.

  1. Read tokens.json, and write luminance and contrast from the formula in Book 1.
  2. List the pairs that are really used together, as [foreground, background] tuples of semantic names.
  3. For each theme and each pair, resolve both names to colours, compute the ratio, and print it with ✓ or ✗.
  4. Set process.exitCode = 1 if any pair is below 4.5, so that npm run check and CI stop.
  5. Add it to your check script, then try to break it: make text-muted lighter in the light theme and watch it fail.

Hint: this is how a design system stays accessible as it grows: not by people remembering the rules, but by a check that runs every time anything changes.

check-contrast.ts
import { readFileSync } from "node:fs";

type Tokens = { primitive: Record<string, string>; semantic: Record<string, Record<string, string>> };
const tokens = JSON.parse(readFileSync(new URL("../tokens.json", import.meta.url), "utf8")) as Tokens;

// The WCAG formula from Book 1
function luminance(hex: string): number {
  const [r, g, b] = [1, 3, 5].map((i) => {
    const c = parseInt(hex.slice(i, i + 2), 16) / 255;
    return c <= 0.03928 ? c / 12.92 : ((c + 0.055) / 1.055) ** 2.4;
  }) as [number, number, number];
  return 0.2126 * r + 0.7152 * g + 0.0722 * b;
}

function contrast(a: string, b: string): number {
  const [hi, lo] = [luminance(a), luminance(b)].sort((x, y) => y - x) as [number, number];
  return (hi + 0.05) / (lo + 0.05);
}

// Each text colour is checked on the background it is used on
const PAIRS: [string, string][] = [
  ["text", "surface"],
  ["text-muted", "surface"],
  ["action-text", "action"],
  ["action-soft-text", "action-soft"],
  ["danger", "danger-soft"],
  ["success", "success-soft"],
  ["warning", "warning-soft"],
];

let failures = 0;
for (const [theme, colours] of Object.entries(tokens.semantic)) {
  for (const [fg, bg] of PAIRS) {
    const ratio = contrast(tokens.primitive[colours[fg]!]!, tokens.primitive[colours[bg]!]!);
    const ok = ratio >= 4.5;
    if (!ok) failures++;
    console.log(`${theme.padEnd(5)} ${`${fg} / ${bg}`.padEnd(32)} ${ratio.toFixed(2).padStart(5)} ${ok ? "✓" : "✗ below 4.5"}`);
  }
}
console.log(failures === 0 ? "All pairs pass WCAG AA." : `${failures} pairs fail. Fix the tokens before releasing.`);
process.exitCode = failures === 0 ? 0 : 1;

Your result should look like this:

Terminal
light text / surface                   16.78 ✓
light text-muted / surface              6.80 ✓
light action-text / action              7.90 ✓
light action-soft-text / action-soft   10.22 ✓
light danger / danger-soft              5.91 ✓
light success / success-soft            5.21 ✓
light warning / warning-soft            6.84 ✓
dark  text / surface                   16.56 ✓
dark  text-muted / surface              7.34 ✓
dark  action-text / action              5.73 ✓
dark  action-soft-text / action-soft   10.22 ✓
dark  danger / danger-soft              5.91 ✓
dark  success / success-soft            5.21 ✓
dark  warning / warning-soft            6.84 ✓
All pairs pass WCAG AA.

Exercises

  1. Easy Add a space-8: 32px token and a radius-lg: 16px token, run the script, and use the new radius for cards.
  2. Easy Add a primitive token whose semantic name is misspelled in the dark theme, run the script, and read the error. Then fix it.
  3. Medium Add a third theme, [data-theme="contrast"], with black text on white, a dark blue action colour and a thick yellow focus colour. Which components needed changes? (The right answer is “none”.)
  4. Medium Add an iconStart slot to the button: a prop that accepts any icon element and places it before the text, with the correct gap in both directions.
  5. Challenge Add motion tokens, duration-fast: 150ms and duration-normal: 250ms, generate them with the script, and use them for the switch’s movement. Then make every duration 0ms inside @media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce), by changing only the generated CSS, not the components.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 11

Book 1 introduced accessibility. In the next chapter you will learn the standard that professionals work to, WCAG, and how to meet it in real component code: focus management in dialogs, live announcements, accessible names, and automatic checks in your tests.

11

Chapter 11

Accessibility (WCAG) in Practice

In this chapter you will learn to

  • read WCAG: its four principles, its levels, and how teams use it
  • give every control an accessible name and the right role
  • manage focus in dialogs, menus and route changes
  • announce changes that happen without a page load
  • run automatic checks with axe-core, and know what they cannot see
  • test with the keyboard and a screen reader like a professional

About one person in six lives with a disability. Some cannot see the screen and listen to it with a screen reader; some cannot use a mouse; some see colours differently, need large text, or are confused by movement. Many more have a temporary or situational limit: a broken arm, bright sunlight, a baby on one arm. Book 1 showed the basics. This chapter turns them into a professional practice, with the standard the whole industry uses, and code that meets it in real components.

Bit

In many countries accessibility is the law, for example through the European Accessibility Act since 2025, and big clients ask for it in contracts. But the best reason is simpler: an app that works for everyone has more users, and is usually better for all of them.

11.1 WCAG

The Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG), published by the W3C, are the standard that laws and contracts refer to. They are organised under four principles, remembered as POUR:

PrincipleMeaningExamples
PerceivableEveryone can perceive the contentText for images, captions for videos, enough contrast, text that can be enlarged
OperableEveryone can use the controlsEverything works with a keyboard, visible focus, enough time, no flashing
UnderstandableEveryone can understand itThe page’s language is declared, clear labels and errors, predictable behaviour
RobustIt works with assistive technologyValid roles, names and states, so screen readers can describe each control

Each principle contains testable success criteria, each at a level: A (the minimum), AA (what laws and most companies require) and AAA (extra, for special cases). “WCAG 2.2 AA” is the usual target, and it is the one this chapter works to.

11.2 What a machine can check

axe-core is the open-source engine behind most accessibility checkers, in browsers, in CI and in test runners. This script opens a page in a real browser, runs axe on it, and prints every problem:

audit-lib.ts
import { createRequire } from "node:module";
import { resolve } from "node:path";
import { pathToFileURL } from "node:url";

const require = createRequire(import.meta.url);
// Only the few Playwright functions this script uses
type Page = {
  goto(url: string): Promise<unknown>;
  addScriptTag(options: { path: string }): Promise<unknown>;
  evaluate<T>(fn: () => T | Promise<T>): Promise<T>;
};
type Browser = { newPage(): Promise<Page>; close(): Promise<void> };
const { chromium } = require("playwright") as { chromium: { launch(): Promise<Browser> } };
const axePath = require.resolve("axe-core/axe.min.js");

type Violation = { id: string; impact: string; help: string; nodes: unknown[] };

// Opens a page (a path from the current folder) in a real browser, runs axe-core, and prints what fails
export async function audit(page: string): Promise<number> {
  const browser = await chromium.launch();
  const tab = await browser.newPage();
  await tab.goto(pathToFileURL(resolve(page)).href);
  await tab.addScriptTag({ path: axePath });
  const violations = (await tab.evaluate(() =>
    // @ts-expect-error axe is added to the page by the script tag above
    axe.run({ runOnly: ["wcag2a", "wcag2aa", "wcag21aa", "wcag22aa", "best-practice"] }).then((r) => r.violations),
  )) as Violation[];
  await browser.close();

  for (const v of violations) {
    console.log(`[${v.impact}] ${v.id}: ${v.help} (${v.nodes.length})`);
  }
  return violations.length;
}

Here is a page that looks fine to a sighted mouse user:

before.html
  ...
  <body>
    <header>
      <div class="brand">Bit's shop</div>
      <input placeholder="Search products…">
      <button class="icon"><svg width="18" height="18" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2.5"><circle cx="11" cy="11" r="7"/><path d="m20 20-4-4"/></svg></button>
    </header>
    <div class="card">
      <img src="mug.svg" width="120" height="80">
      <div>
        <h4>New: the Bit mug</h4>
        <p class="faint">Big enough for long coding nights. Only this week: 20% off.</p>
        <a href="#mug">Click here</a>
      </div>
    </div>
    <div class="card">
      <h4>Newsletter</h4>
      <input type="email">
      <div onclick="alert('ok')"><button tabindex="-1">Join</button></div>
    </div>
  </body>
  ...
Terminal
[critical] button-name: Buttons must have discernible text (1)
[serious] color-contrast: Elements must meet minimum color contrast ratio thresholds (1)
[serious] html-has-lang: <html> element must have a lang attribute (1)
[critical] image-alt: Images must have alternative text (1)
[critical] label: Form elements must have labels (1)
[moderate] landmark-one-main: Document should have one main landmark (1)
[moderate] page-has-heading-one: Page should contain a level-one heading (1)
[moderate] region: All page content should be contained by landmarks (3)
Before: 8 issues

Each line names the rule, how serious it is, and how many elements fail. Look up any rule by its id to see why it matters and how to fix it:

  • button-name: the search button contains only an icon, so a screen reader says just “button”. Give it aria-label="Search", and hide the icon with aria-hidden="true".
  • image-alt: the picture has no alt. A screen reader may read the file name, “mug dot svg”.
  • label: the email field has no label, so nobody knows what to type.
  • color-contrast: the grey text is too light. The minimum is 4.5:1 for normal text.
  • html-has-lang: without lang, a screen reader may read Arabic with an English voice.
  • The last three are about structure: there is no main landmark and no h1, so screen reader users cannot jump to the content or see the outline of the page.

Now read the list again, and notice what axe did not report. The link says “Click here”, which means nothing out of context. The “Join” button is unreachable by keyboard, because it has tabindex="-1" and the real click handler is on a div. And the search field has only a placeholder, which axe accepts as a name, though it disappears as soon as the user types. Automatic tools find roughly a third of real problems. They are essential, and never enough.

Professional habit: three layers of testing

Automatic checks on every change, in CI and in component tests. A keyboard pass on every new screen: Tab through everything, use it all, and never lose sight of the focus. A screen reader pass on important flows before release: NVDA with Firefox or Chrome on Windows, VoiceOver with Safari on Mac and iPhone, and TalkBack on Android.

11.3 Names, roles and states

Assistive technology describes each control with three facts: its role (button, link, checkbox, tab), its accessible name (“Search”, “Delete account”) and its state (pressed, expanded, checked, invalid). Native HTML elements provide all three. The rules, in order of preference:

  1. Use the native element: <button>, <a href>, <input type="checkbox">, <dialog>. Keyboard support, role and state come free.
  2. Name it with visible text whenever possible. The text users see is the name they will say to voice control software.
  3. Use ARIA only when HTML has no element for the job, such as tabs or menus, and then follow the pattern in the WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices exactly. The first rule of ARIA is: no ARIA is better than bad ARIA.

The fixed page applies all of these, and passes:

after.html
  ...
  <body>
    <header>
      <p class="brand">Bit's shop</p>
      <form role="search" style="display: flex; gap: 8px; align-items: center">
        <label for="q" class="sr-only">Search</label>
        <input id="q" type="search" placeholder="Search products…">
        <button class="icon" aria-label="Search"><svg aria-hidden="true" width="18" height="18" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2.5"><circle cx="11" cy="11" r="7"/><path d="m20 20-4-4"/></svg></button>
      </form>
    </header>
    <main>
      <h1 class="sr-only">Bit's shop</h1>
      <section class="card">
        <img src="mug.svg" width="120" height="80" alt="A white mug with the purple Bit robot on it">
        <div>
          <h2>New: the Bit mug</h2>
          <p class="faint">Big enough for long coding nights. Only this week: 20% off.</p>
          <a href="#mug">Read about the Bit mug</a>
        </div>
      </section>
      <section class="card">
        <h2>Newsletter</h2>
        <form style="display: flex; gap: 8px; align-items: center">
          <label for="email">Email</label>
          <input id="email" type="email" autocomplete="email" dir="ltr">
          <button type="submit">Join</button>
        </form>
      </section>
    </main>
  </body>
  ...
Terminal
No violations found.

Quick check

  1. Which WCAG level do most laws and companies require?
  2. An icon-only button reads as just “button”. What two attributes fix it?
  3. A page passes axe with no violations. Is it accessible?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

11.4 Managing focus

On a classic website, every click loads a new page, and the browser puts the focus at the top. In a single-page app, content appears and disappears without a page load, so your code must decide where the keyboard focus goes. Three moments matter most:

Dialogs

When a dialog opens, the focus must move into it, stay inside it while it is open, close on Escape, and return to the button that opened it. The native <dialog> element, opened with showModal(), does all of this, and also makes the page behind it inert: unreachable by the keyboard and invisible to screen readers.

ConfirmDialog.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { useTemplateRef, watchEffect } from "vue";

const open = defineModel<boolean>({ required: true });
defineProps<{ title: string; confirmLabel: string; cancelLabel: string }>();
const emit = defineEmits<{ confirm: [] }>();
const dialog = useTemplateRef<HTMLDialogElement>("dialog");

watchEffect(() => {
  const el = dialog.value;
  if (!el) return;
  if (open.value && !el.open) el.showModal(); // modal: traps focus, Esc closes, the page behind is inert
  if (!open.value && el.open) el.close(); // the browser returns the focus to the button that opened it
});
</script>

<template>
  <dialog ref="dialog" aria-labelledby="dlg-title" aria-describedby="dlg-text" @close="open = false">
    <h2 id="dlg-title">{{ title }}</h2>
    <p id="dlg-text"><slot /></p>
    <div class="row">
      <button type="button" class="secondary" autofocus @click="open = false">{{ cancelLabel }}</button>
      <button type="button" class="danger" @click="emit('confirm'), (open = false)">{{ confirmLabel }}</button>
    </div>
  </dialog>
</template>

Notice two choices. The dialog is named by its title (aria-labelledby) and described by its text (aria-describedby), so a screen reader reads both when it opens. And for a destructive action, the safe button gets the first focus.

Route changes

When the router shows a new page, a screen reader user hears nothing: no page loaded. After each navigation, move the focus to the new page’s h1 (give it tabindex="-1" so it can receive focus), and update document.title. Both tell the user where they are.

Things that disappear

If the focused element is removed, for example a “Delete” button in a list row that is deleted, the focus falls back to the top of the page. Move it somewhere sensible first: the next row, or the list’s heading.

11.5 Announcing changes

Changes that appear without a focus move, such as “3 results”, “Saved” or a new notification, must be announced through a live region: an element with role="status" (polite: waits for a pause) or role="alert" (assertive: interrupts). You built both in the earlier chapters: the error summary of the forms chapter and the notifications of the global state chapter. Two rules make live regions work:

  • The region must exist in the page before the text appears in it. A region created together with its message is often not announced.
  • Announce results, not every keystroke. A search box that announces after each letter is unusable.

11.6 More criteria that catch teams out

CriterionWhat it asksIn practice
Reflow (1.4.10)Content works at 320px wide without horizontal scrollingTest at 400% zoom; this is the mobile-first layout of Book 1
Text spacing (1.4.12)Nothing breaks when users increase line and letter spacingNo fixed heights on text containers
Focus visible and not obscured (2.4.7, 2.4.11)The focus can always be seen, not hidden under a sticky header:focus-visible styles, and scroll-margin for sticky bars
