fmt("ar-EG", { dateStyle: "full", timeStyle: "short" });
fmt("ar-SA-u-ca-islamic-umalqura", { dateStyle: "long" });

const rtf = new Intl.RelativeTimeFormat("ar", { numeric: "auto" });
console.log([-1, 0, 1, 2, 3, 11].map((d) => rtf.format(d, "day")).join(" | "));
console.log(new Intl.ListFormat("ar", { type: "conjunction" }).format(["HTML", "CSS", "JavaScript"]));
Terminal
en-GB                      Friday, 20 March 2026 at 16:30
en-US                      Friday, March 20, 2026 at 4:30 PM
ar-EG                      الجمعة، ٢٠ مارس ٢٠٢٦ في ٤:٣٠ م
ar-SA-u-ca-islamic-umalqura ١ شوال ١٤٤٧ هـ
أمس | اليوم | غدًا | بعد الغد | خلال 3 أيام | خلال 11 يومًا
HTML وCSS وJavaScript

Dates follow the same idea, including the order of day and month, the names of days, and whole calendars: the extension -u-ca-islamic-umalqura shows that this day, 20 March 2026, is 1 Shawwal 1447, the first day of Eid al-Fitr. RelativeTimeFormat writes “yesterday” and “in 3 days” with the correct grammar, and ListFormat joins words with the right “and”. Store dates in UTC, and format them in the user’s time zone only when you show them.

15.2 Plurals

English needs two forms: “1 item”, “2 items”. Arabic needs six, and the rules depend on the number in ways that are easy to get wrong. Intl.PluralRules knows them:

plurals.ts
// Arabic has six plural categories; English has two
const ar = new Intl.PluralRules("ar");
const en = new Intl.PluralRules("en");

for (const n of [0, 1, 2, 3, 10, 11, 100, 101]) {
  console.log(String(n).padStart(3), "ar:", ar.select(n).padEnd(5), " en:", en.select(n));
}
console.log("ar categories:", ar.resolvedOptions().pluralCategories.join(", "));
Terminal
  0 ar: zero   en: other
  1 ar: one    en: one
  2 ar: two    en: other
  3 ar: few    en: other
 10 ar: few    en: other
 11 ar: many   en: other
100 ar: other  en: other
101 ar: other  en: other
ar categories: few, many, one, two, zero, other

A message catalogue therefore stores a plural message as one text per category, and the code picks the right one. Here is a small catalogue and translation function, the core of what libraries such as i18next, FormatJS and vue-i18n do:

messages.ts
// Every message has a key, and placeholders in braces
export const messages = {
  en: {
    "cart.title": "Your cart",
    "cart.items": { one: "{count} item", other: "{count} items" },
    "cart.total": "Total: {total}",
    "cart.empty": "Your cart is empty.",
  },
  ar: {
    "cart.title": "سلتك",
    "cart.items": {
      zero: "لا عناصر",
      one: "عنصر واحد",
      two: "عنصران",
      few: "{count} عناصر",
      many: "{count} عنصراً",
      other: "{count} عنصر",
    },
    "cart.total": "المجموع: {total}",
    "cart.empty": "سلتك فارغة.",
  },
} as const;

export type Locale = keyof typeof messages;
type Plural = Partial<Record<Intl.LDMLPluralRule, string>>;

export function translate(locale: Locale, key: keyof (typeof messages)["en"], values: Record<string, string | number> = {}) {
  const entry = messages[locale][key] as string | Plural;
  const text =
    typeof entry === "string"
      ? entry
      : (entry[new Intl.PluralRules(locale).select(Number(values.count ?? 0))] ?? entry.other ?? "");
  const format = new Intl.NumberFormat(locale);
  return text.replace(/\{(\w+)\}/g, (_, name: string) => {
    const value = values[name];
    return typeof value === "number" ? format.format(value) : String(value ?? "");
  });
}
translate.ts
import { translate } from "./messages";

for (const n of [0, 1, 2, 5, 11, 100]) {
  console.log(String(n).padStart(3), "|", translate("ar", "cart.items", { count: n }).padEnd(12), "|", translate("en", "cart.items", { count: n }));
}
Terminal
  0 | لا عناصر     | 0 items
  1 | عنصر واحد    | 1 item
  2 | عنصران       | 2 items
  5 | 5 عناصر      | 5 items
 11 | 11 عنصراً    | 11 items
100 | 100 عنصر     | 100 items
  • Every text has a key such as cart.items. Code never contains user-visible words, so translators can work without touching it.
  • Values are passed into placeholders, never glued together with +. Word order differs between languages: “Total: 335” may become “335 :المجموع” in another, and only the translator knows.
  • Numbers inside messages are formatted with the locale’s rules, too.

Watch out: never build sentences from pieces

"You have " + n + " " + (n === 1 ? "item" : "items") cannot be translated: Arabic needs six forms, other languages change the word order or the gender. Give the translator the whole sentence with a placeholder, and let plural rules choose.

Quick check

  1. Which locale shows Arabic text with Latin digits in Egypt?
  2. Which Arabic plural category does the number 11 belong to?
  3. Why should translations use placeholders instead of joining strings with +?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

15.3 Layouts that mirror

In a right-to-left page, almost the whole layout mirrors: the menu moves to the right, text aligns right, the “back” arrow points right. If your CSS says left and right, you must write every rule twice. Logical properties describe positions relative to the text direction instead, and you write each rule once:

Physical (avoid)Logical (use)
margin-left, padding-rightmargin-inline-start, padding-inline-end
left: 0, right: 0inset-inline-start: 0, inset-inline-end: 0
text-align: lefttext-align: start
border-leftborder-inline-start
width, heightinline-size, block-size (optional)

Flexbox and grid already follow the direction. Only a few things need explicit care: icons that show a direction, such as arrows, “next” and “back”, which are flipped with transform: scaleX(-1) under [dir="rtl"]; and things that must not flip, such as a play button, a clock, a logo, or a graph’s time axis.

In Vue, the current language is app-wide state that changes rarely: exactly the job of provide and inject (the global state chapter). provideI18n sets lang and dir on the page, and gives every component a t function and a money formatter. Both are computed, so every text on the screen follows the language when it changes:

i18n.ts
import { computed, inject, provide, ref, watchEffect, type ComputedRef, type InjectionKey, type Ref } from "vue";
import { translate, type Locale } from "../messages";

type I18n = {
  locale: Ref<Locale>;
  t: ComputedRef<(key: Parameters<typeof translate>[1], values?: Record<string, string | number>) => string>;
  money: ComputedRef<(value: number) => string>;
};

const I18nKey: InjectionKey<I18n> = Symbol("i18n");

export function provideI18n(initial: Locale) {
  const locale = ref<Locale>(initial);

  watchEffect(() => {
    document.documentElement.lang = locale.value;
    document.documentElement.dir = locale.value === "ar" ? "rtl" : "ltr";
  });

  const t = computed(() => (key: Parameters<typeof translate>[1], values?: Record<string, string | number>) =>
    translate(locale.value, key, values),
  );
  const money = computed(() => {
    const currency = new Intl.NumberFormat(locale.value === "ar" ? "ar-EG" : "en-EG", { style: "currency", currency: "EGP" });
    return (v: number) => currency.format(v);
  });

  provide(I18nKey, { locale, t, money });
}

export function useI18n(): I18n {
  const value = inject(I18nKey);
  if (!value) throw new Error("useI18n() must be used inside a component that called provideI18n()");
  return value;
}
Cart.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { useI18n } from "./i18n";

const LINES = [
  { name: "Code from Zero", price: 250 },
  { name: "Bit mug", price: 85 },
];
const { t, money, locale } = useI18n();
const total = LINES.reduce((sum, l) => sum + l.price, 0);
</script>

<template>
  <section class="card">
    <h2>
      {{ t("cart.title") }}
      <span class="badge">{{ t("cart.items", { count: LINES.length }) }}</span>
    </h2>
    <div v-for="l in LINES" :key="l.name" class="row">
      <bdi>{{ l.name }}</bdi>
      <span>{{ money(l.price) }}</span>
    </div>
    <p><b>{{ t("cart.total", { total: money(total) }) }}</b></p>
    <button type="button" class="next" @click="locale = locale === 'ar' ? 'en' : 'ar'">
      {{ locale === "ar" ? "English" : "العربية" }}
    </button>
  </section>
</template>

15.4 Bidirectional text

When Arabic and English mix in one line, the browser uses the Unicode bidirectional algorithm to decide the order. It usually works, but numbers, punctuation and English words next to the edges of a sentence often land in surprising places:

bidi.html
  ...
  <body>
    <div class="cards">
      <div class="card bad">
        <p>طلب <span>#1042</span> من <span>Sara Khan</span> (3 items)</p>
        <p>الهاتف: <span>+20 100 123 4567</span></p>
        <p>البريد: <span>sara@example.com</span></p>
      </div>
      <div class="card good">
        <p>طلب <bdi>#1042</bdi> من <bdi>Sara Khan</bdi> (<bdi>3 items</bdi>)</p>
        <p>الهاتف: <span dir="ltr">+20 100 123 4567</span></p>
        <p>البريد: <span dir="ltr">sara@example.com</span></p>
      </div>
    </div>
  </body>
  ...
  • <bdi> (bidirectional isolation) wraps any value inserted into a sentence, such as a name, a number, or text from the user, so its direction cannot disturb the rest of the line. In React and Vue, wrap interpolated values in it whenever the language of the value is unknown.
  • dir="ltr" forces left-to-right for things that are always written that way: phone numbers, email addresses, code, URLs.
  • dir="auto" on an input or a comment lets the browser pick the direction from the first strong letter typed.

Professional habit: test with real Arabic, from the first screen

Lorem ipsum hides every RTL bug. Build each screen with real Arabic text, long Arabic words, mixed Arabic and English, and numbers, and look at it in both directions before you call it finished. Arabic text is also often longer or taller than English, so give layouts room.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Text written in componentsTranslation means editing codeMessage catalogues with keys
Sentences joined with +Wrong word order and grammar in other languagesWhole messages with placeholders
n === 1 ? "item" : "items"Wrong Arabic pluralsIntl.PluralRules
Formatting numbers and dates by handWrong separators, digits and orderIntl.NumberFormat, Intl.DateTimeFormat
margin-left, left: 0Layouts that do not mirrorLogical properties
Inserted values without isolationJumbled lines, backwards phone numbers<bdi> and dir="ltr"
Flipping everythingClocks and play buttons point the wrong wayFlip only icons that show a direction

Summary

  • Internationalisation is an architecture decision: no text in code, every format by rules, every layout able to mirror.
  • Intl formats numbers, money, dates, relative times and lists per locale; choose digits and calendars with extensions such as -u-nu-latn and -u-ca-islamic-umalqura.
  • Arabic has six plural categories; let Intl.PluralRules choose the right text.
  • Keep texts in catalogues with keys and placeholders, never in code or glued pieces.
  • Logical CSS properties mirror layouts automatically; flip only directional icons.
  • Isolate inserted values with <bdi>, and force dir="ltr" for phone numbers, emails and code.

Quick check answers: 1. ar-EG-u-nu-latn. 2. many. 3. Because word order and grammar differ between languages; only the translator, seeing the whole sentence, can place the value correctly.

Practice projects

Test every project in both languages, with real Arabic text, and with numbers that hit every plural category: 0, 1, 2, 3, 11 and 100.

Project 15.1 · Easy

A catalogue with real Arabic plurals

Build a small message catalogue and a translate function that picks the right plural form and formats numbers for each language.

You will practise: keys, placeholders, Intl.PluralRules, Intl.NumberFormat, and types derived from the catalogue.

  1. Write the catalogue as one object per language. Plural messages are objects with one text per category.
  2. Give Arabic all six categories (zero, one, two, few, many, other), and English two.
  3. In translate, pick the category with new Intl.PluralRules(locale).select(count), falling back to other.
  4. Replace {name} placeholders with values, formatting numbers with Intl.NumberFormat(locale).
  5. Derive the type of the keys from the English catalogue, so a misspelled key is a compile error.

Hint: “zero” and “one” in Arabic are often whole phrases without a number at all (“لا عناصر”, “عنصر واحد”), which is why each category gets its own full text.

Terminal
  0 | لا عناصر     | 0 items
  1 | عنصر واحد    | 1 item
  2 | عنصران       | 2 items
  5 | 5 عناصر      | 5 items
 11 | 11 عنصراً    | 11 items
100 | 100 عنصر     | 100 items

Project 15.2 · Medium

One app, two directions

A cart that switches between English and Arabic at runtime, mirrors its layout, and formats money and plurals correctly in both.

You will practise: a language context, setting lang and dir, logical CSS, flipping directional icons, and <bdi>.

  1. Write a provider that keeps the locale, sets document.documentElement.lang and dir in an effect, and provides t and a money formatter.
  2. Write all spacing and borders with logical properties, and check that nothing needs a special RTL rule.
  3. Flip the arrow icon only, with [dir="rtl"] .next::after { transform: scaleX(-1); }.
  4. Wrap product names, which may be in either language, in <bdi>.
  5. Switch languages back and forth, and check every line: text, plural, prices, alignment, arrow.

Hint: save the chosen language (in localStorage or the address, such as /ar/cart) and start from navigator.languages on the first visit. An address per language is also what search engines need.

Project 15.3 · Challenge

A translation checker

Compare two message catalogues and report keys that are missing, keys that are extra, and messages whose placeholders do not match, so broken translations cannot reach users.

You will practise: reading JSON, regular expressions with matchAll, set-like comparisons, and failing the build.

  1. Load en.json (the source of truth) and ar.json.
  2. For each English key, report it if Arabic lacks it.
  3. Extract the placeholders of both texts with /\{(\w+)\}/g, sort them, and compare. A translator who renamed {total} to {sum} would show an empty value to users.
  4. Report Arabic keys that English no longer has: old texts that should be deleted.
  5. Set a failing exit code when there is any problem, and add the script to npm run check.

Hint: real projects also check plural messages (every category the language needs is present) and keys used in the code but missing from the catalogue. Libraries such as i18next have tools for both.

check-messages.ts
import { readFileSync } from "node:fs";

// Compare the two catalogues: missing keys, extra keys, and placeholders that do not match
const load = (file: string) => JSON.parse(readFileSync(new URL(file, import.meta.url), "utf8")) as Record<string, string>;
const en = load("./en.json");
const ar = load("./ar.json");
const placeholders = (text: string) => [...text.matchAll(/\{(\w+)\}/g)].map((m) => m[1]).sort().join(",");

let problems = 0;
for (const key of Object.keys(en)) {
  if (!(key in ar)) {
    console.log(`✗ ${key}: missing in ar`);
    problems++;
  } else if (placeholders(en[key]!) !== placeholders(ar[key]!)) {
    console.log(`✗ ${key}: placeholders differ: en {${placeholders(en[key]!)}} ar {${placeholders(ar[key]!)}}`);
    problems++;
  }
}
for (const key of Object.keys(ar)) {
  if (!(key in en)) {
    console.log(`✗ ${key}: not in en`);
    problems++;
  }
}
console.log(problems === 0 ? "All keys match." : `${problems} problems`);
process.exitCode = problems ? 1 : 0;
Terminal
✗ nav.cart: missing in ar
✗ cart.total: placeholders differ: en {total} ar {sum}
✗ profile.old: not in en
3 problems

Exercises

  1. Easy Add fr-FR, hi-IN and fa-IR to numbers.ts. What is special about each?
  2. Easy Print today’s date in the Gregorian and the Umm al-Qura calendars, in ar-SA and in en-US.
  3. Medium Add a "cart.days" plural message (“arrives in 3 days”) to both languages with all the categories each needs, and print it for 0, 1, 2, 3, 11 and 100.
  4. Medium Find every physical property (left, right, margin-left…) in the CSS of one of your projects, replace it with its logical equivalent, and check the page in both directions.
  5. Challenge Add dir="auto" to a comment input and to each displayed comment. Type an English comment, an Arabic one, and one that starts with a number. Which direction does each get, and why?

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 16

You now know how to build each part of a professional frontend. The last part of the book is about the work of a senior: organising a large codebase so that many people can change it safely, rendering on the server, reviewing code and making technical decisions, and finally a capstone project that brings it all together.

16

Chapter 16

Frontend Architecture at Scale

In this chapter you will learn to

  • explain what architecture is for: keeping change cheap as the app and the team grow
  • organise code by feature, with clear layers and public entry points
  • enforce the rules automatically, instead of hoping people remember them
  • separate pure logic from the code that talks to the world
  • put all communication with the server behind one typed, validated layer
  • know when monorepos, feature flags and micro-frontends help, and when they hurt
  • record important decisions so the next person understands them

A small app can be organised any way at all, because one person holds it in their head. At fifty screens and ten developers, that stops working: every change touches files nobody understands, two people edit the same file every day, and fixing one bug creates two more. Architecture is the set of decisions that keeps change cheap and safe as the app grows: where code lives, what may depend on what, and how the pieces talk. It is less about clever patterns than about boundaries.

Bit

A good test of an architecture: can a new team member add a small feature in their first week, touching only a few files, without breaking anything else? If yes, the boundaries are working.

16.1 Organise by feature

Beginners often group files by type: all components in one folder, all hooks in another, all API calls in a third. That works until the app grows; then every feature is spread across five folders, and nothing tells you which files belong together. Mature codebases group by feature: everything about the cart lives in features/cart.

Terminal
src/
├── app/
│   ├── App.vue
│   └── routes.ts
├── features/
│   ├── account/
│   │   ├── AccountPage.vue
│   │   └── index.ts
│   ├── cart/
│   │   ├── CartBadge.vue
│   │   ├── index.ts
│   │   └── store.ts
│   └── catalog/
│       ├── CatalogPage.vue
│       ├── index.ts
│       └── useProducts.ts
└── shared/
    ├── api/
    │   └── client.ts
    ├── lib/
    │   └── money.ts
    └── ui/
        ├── AppHeader.vue
        ├── AppLayout.vue
        └── BaseButton.vue

Three layers, each with one job:

  • shared/: code with no knowledge of the business, usable anywhere: the design system components, the HTTP client, formatting helpers.
  • features/: one folder per piece of the product, each with its components, state, API calls and logic, and an index.ts that exports only what other code may use (the barrel of Chapter 3).
  • app/: the composition: routes, layouts and providers that put the features together.

Dependency rules

Folders alone do not make an architecture; the rules between them do. Each layer may import only from layers below it:

  1. Shared code never imports a feature. Otherwise “shared” code is secretly tied to one feature, and cannot move or be reused.
  2. A feature imports another feature only through its index.ts. The rest of the folder is private, so the cart team can reorganise it without breaking the catalogue.
  3. Nothing imports from app/. It is the top; it uses everything, and nothing uses it.

Rules that live only in a document are broken within weeks, by people in a hurry. So professional teams check them automatically, with ESLint rules such as no-restricted-imports or tools like dependency-cruiser. Project 16.3 builds such a checker from scratch: in this sample codebase it finds the two rule-breaking imports hidden in it.

16.2 A functional core, an imperative shell

Inside a feature, the most useful line to draw is between deciding and doing. The functional core is pure logic: given data, it returns data, with no network, no storage and no Vue. The cart’s totals, the discount rules, the reducer of the state chapter. The imperative shell is everything that touches the world: components, effects, API calls, storage.

Keep the core as large and the shell as thin as you can. The core is where the bugs that cost money live, and it is trivial to test with plain unit tests (Chapter 12); the shell then mostly passes data in and out. This is why the composables and stores in this book were tested in Node before Vue ever saw them.

16.3 One door to the server

When fifty components call fetch directly, every one of them invents its own error handling, and a change to the API means a search through the whole codebase. Put all communication with the server behind one API layer:

  • It knows the base address, headers and authentication, in one place.
  • It validates every response with a schema (the forms chapter), so wrong data stops at the door instead of crashing a screen later.
  • It turns every failure into a small set of typed errors the screens can handle: not found, invalid, server error, network.

Project 16.2 builds one, and shows it handling a good answer, a 404, a price sent as a string, and a server that never answers. Server-state libraries such as TanStack Query then call these functions; they cache, but the API layer decides what is valid.

Quick check

  1. The account feature needs the cart badge. Which file should it import from?
  2. Why should shared/ never import from features/?
  3. Where do discount calculations belong: in a component, or in the functional core?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

16.4 Bigger structures

ToolWhat it isWhen it helpsCost
MonorepoSeveral apps and packages in one repository, with tools such as pnpm workspaces and TurborepoA web app, an admin app and a design system that change togetherBuild tooling to learn and maintain
Feature flagsSwitches that turn features on for some users, decided at run timeReleasing unfinished work safely, gradual rollouts, A/B testsOld flags must be removed, or the code fills with dead branches
Micro-frontendsSeparate apps by separate teams, combined in the browserVery large organisations where teams must release independentlyDuplicate libraries, inconsistent design, complex debugging; rarely worth it below dozens of developers

Choose the simplest structure that solves a problem you actually have. A well-organised single app with clear feature boundaries serves most teams for years.

16.5 Record your decisions

Six months from now, someone will ask why the app uses Pinia and not Vuex, or why dates are stored in UTC. If the answer is only in someone’s head, they may undo a good decision. An Architecture Decision Record (ADR) is a short file in the repository, written when a significant decision is made:

docs/adr/0007-server-state-with-tanstack-query.md
# 7. Server state with TanStack Query

Date: 2026-03-12  ·  Status: accepted

## Context
Screens fetch data in effects. We have duplicate requests, no caching,
and loading and error states handled differently on every screen.

## Decision
All server data is read through TanStack Query, calling the typed
functions of shared/api. Effects no longer call fetch.

## Consequences
+ Caching, deduplication and retries everywhere, for free.
+ One way to show loading and errors.
- One more library to learn; existing screens migrate over two sprints.

Professional habit: make the right thing the easy thing

Good architecture is not enforced by reviews alone. Provide templates for new features, a lint rule for every boundary, and a clear example of each pattern in the codebase. People copy what they see; make sure what they see is right.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Folders by type in a large appEvery feature spread everywhereFolders by feature
Importing another feature’s internal filesChanging one feature breaks anotherPublic index.ts, enforced by a checker
“Shared” code that imports featuresHidden coupling; nothing can moveDependencies point down only
fetch in every componentInconsistent errors; API changes everywhereOne typed, validated API layer
Business rules inside componentsHard to test and to reuseA pure functional core
Adopting micro-frontends too earlyComplexity without benefitStart with one app and clear boundaries
Decisions that live in someone’s headGood choices get undoneShort ADRs in the repository

Summary

  • Architecture keeps change cheap and safe as the app and team grow; it is mostly about boundaries.
  • Organise by feature, in three layers: shared, features, app. Dependencies point down; features talk through their public index.ts.
  • Enforce the rules with tools, not memory.
  • Keep a large, pure functional core and a thin imperative shell.
  • One API layer talks to the server, validates responses, and returns typed errors.
  • Use monorepos, feature flags and micro-frontends only for problems you really have.
  • Record significant decisions in short ADRs.

Quick check answers: 1. From features/cart/index.ts, the cart’s public entry point, not from features/cart/CartBadge.vue. 2. Because it would tie the “shared” code to one feature, so it could no longer be reused or moved, and changes to the feature would break unrelated screens. 3. In the functional core: it is pure logic that should be tested on its own.

Practice projects

These projects are about the code around features: the shape of folders, the door to the server, and the tools that keep both honest. Apply them to the biggest project you have built in this book.

Project 16.1 · Easy

From folders by type to folders by feature

Reorganise one of your projects into shared, features and app, with a public index.ts for every feature.

You will practise: deciding what is shared and what belongs to a feature, barrels, and moving files without breaking imports.

  1. List every file and write next to it the feature it belongs to, or “shared” if it knows nothing about the business.
  2. Create the new folders and move the files with your editor’s rename command, which updates imports for you.
  3. Give each feature an index.ts that exports only what other code needs.
  4. Change imports from other features to point at those index.ts files.
  5. Run npm run check and the tests: a reorganisation must not change behaviour.

Hint: when you cannot decide whether something is shared, keep it in the feature. Moving code up to shared later, when a second feature needs it, is easy; untangling a “shared” folder full of business logic is not.

Terminal
src/
├── app/
│   ├── App.vue
│   └── routes.ts
├── features/
│   ├── account/
│   │   ├── AccountPage.vue
│   │   └── index.ts
│   ├── cart/
│   │   ├── CartBadge.vue
│   │   ├── index.ts
│   │   └── store.ts
│   └── catalog/
│       ├── CatalogPage.vue
│       ├── index.ts
│       └── useProducts.ts
└── shared/
    ├── api/
    │   └── client.ts
    ├── lib/
    │   └── money.ts
    └── ui/
        ├── AppHeader.vue
        ├── AppLayout.vue
        └── BaseButton.vue

Project 16.2 · Medium

A typed API layer

Write the one module that talks to the server: it validates every answer and turns every failure into a typed error the screens can handle.

You will practise: Zod schemas, discriminated unions for errors, AbortSignal.timeout, dependency injection of fetch, and a local test server.

  1. Describe each resource with a schema, and derive its type with z.infer.
  2. Define ApiError as a union of network, not-found, invalid and server, and Result<T> as success or error.
  3. Write one private get(path, schema) that fetches with a timeout, checks the status, parses the JSON with the schema, and never throws.
  4. Export one small function per endpoint, such as products() and product(id).
  5. Accept fetch as a parameter, so tests can pass a fake; and test against a local server that answers well, with 404, with bad data, and too slowly.

Hint: look at the third answer: the server sent the price as a string. Without validation, that would have shown up as “8EGP”, or as NaN in a total, far from its cause. With it, the error names the exact field.

shared/api/api.ts
import { z } from "zod";

// One place that talks to the server: typed, validated, and with errors the screens can understand
export const Product = z.object({ id: z.number(), name: z.string(), price: z.number().nonnegative() });
export type Product = z.infer<typeof Product>;

export type ApiError =
  | { kind: "network"; message: string }
  | { kind: "not-found"; path: string }
  | { kind: "invalid"; issues: string[] }
  | { kind: "server"; status: number };

export type Result<T> = { ok: true; data: T } | { ok: false; error: ApiError };

export function createApi(base: string, fetcher: typeof fetch = fetch) {
  async function get<T>(path: string, schema: z.ZodType<T>): Promise<Result<T>> {
    let response: Response;
    try {
      response = await fetcher(base + path, { signal: AbortSignal.timeout(2000) });
    } catch (e) {
      return { ok: false, error: { kind: "network", message: (e as Error).name } };
    }
    if (response.status === 404) return { ok: false, error: { kind: "not-found", path } };
    if (!response.ok) return { ok: false, error: { kind: "server", status: response.status } };
    const parsed = schema.safeParse(await response.json());
    if (!parsed.success) {
      return { ok: false, error: { kind: "invalid", issues: parsed.error.issues.map((i) => `${i.path.join(".")}: ${i.message}`) } };
    }
    return { ok: true, data: parsed.data };
  }

  return {
    products: () => get("/products", z.array(Product)),
    product: (id: number) => get(`/products/${id}`, Product),
  };
}
Terminal
✓ Products: [{"id":1,"name":"Code from Zero","price":25}]
✗ {"kind":"not-found","path":"/products/9"}
✗ {"kind":"invalid","issues":["price: Invalid input: expected number, received string"]}
✗ {"kind":"network","message":"TimeoutError"}

Project 16.3 · Challenge

A boundary checker

Write a script that reads every import in the codebase, works out which layer and feature each file belongs to, and reports imports that break the dependency rules.

You will practise: walking folders, regular expressions on source code, resolving relative paths, and turning rules into checks with exit codes.

  1. Walk src and collect every .ts and .vue file.
  2. Write where(path), which splits a path into its layer (app, features, shared), its feature, and the rest.
  3. For every relative import, resolve the target with path.resolve and compare the two locations against the three rules.
  4. Print each violation with the file, the import and the rule it breaks; exit with 1 if there are any.
  5. Run it on the sample: it finds the header that reaches into the cart store, and the account page that bypasses the cart’s index.ts.

Hint: a regular expression is enough for a checker like this, but real tools use the TypeScript compiler to read imports exactly, including aliases such as @/features/cart. Start simple, and switch when the simple version stops being right.

boundaries.ts
import { readdirSync, readFileSync, statSync } from "node:fs";
import { dirname, join, relative, resolve } from "node:path";

// The layers, from the bottom up: each may import only from layers below it: shared → features → app
const root = resolve(process.argv[2] ?? "sample/src");

function files(dir: string): string[] {
  return readdirSync(dir).flatMap((name) => {
    const path = join(dir, name);
    return statSync(path).isDirectory() ? files(path) : /\.(ts|vue)$/.test(name) ? [path] : [];
  });
}

// "features/cart/store.ts" -> { layer: "features", feature: "cart", rest: "store.ts" }
function where(path: string) {
  const [layer = "", feature = "", ...rest] = relative(root, path).split("/");
  return { layer, feature: layer === "features" ? feature : "", rest: rest.join("/") };
}

const problems: string[] = [];
for (const file of files(root)) {
  const from = where(file);
  for (const [, target] of readFileSync(file, "utf8").matchAll(/from\s+"(\.[^"]+)"/g)) {
    const to = where(resolve(dirname(file), target!));
    const at = `${relative(root, file)} → ${target}`;
    if (from.layer === "shared" && to.layer === "features") problems.push(`${at}\n    shared code must not import a feature`);
    if (to.layer === "app" && from.layer !== "app") problems.push(`${at}\n    nothing imports from app/`);
    if (from.layer === "features" && to.layer === "features" && from.feature !== to.feature && to.rest !== "") {
      problems.push(`${at}\n    import another feature only through its index.ts`);
    }
  }
}

for (const p of problems) console.log("✗ " + p);
console.log(problems.length === 0 ? "No boundary violations." : `${problems.length} violations`);
process.exitCode = problems.length ? 1 : 0;
Terminal
✗ features/account/AccountPage.vue → ../cart/CartBadge.vue
    import another feature only through its index.ts
✗ shared/ui/AppHeader.vue → ../../features/cart/store
    shared code must not import a feature
2 violations

Exercises

  1. Easy Draw the folder tree of one of your projects. Is it organised by type or by feature? Sketch the feature version.
  2. Easy Fix the two violations in the sample codebase, and run the checker until it reports none.
  3. Medium Add a fourth rule to the checker: features must not import from app/ either, and code in shared/ui must not import from shared/api.
  4. Medium Write an ADR for a decision you made in one of your projects, such as the choice of a library, with context, decision and consequences.
  5. Challenge Express the same boundary rules as an ESLint configuration with no-restricted-imports patterns, and check that ESLint reports the same two problems in the sample.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 17

Every app in this book so far has been drawn entirely in the browser. In the next chapter the server joins in: pages rendered before they reach the user, for speed and search engines, with a framework built on everything you have learned.

18

Chapter 18

Code Review, Mentoring and Technical Decisions

In this chapter you will learn to

  • open pull requests that are small, focused and easy to review
  • let machines check everything a machine can, before a person looks
  • review code in a useful order, and write comments that are clear and kind
  • receive reviews without taking them personally
  • help less experienced developers grow, through pairing, questions and feedback
  • make technical decisions with a matrix, write them down as decision records, and manage technical debt

Up to now this book has been about the code you write. A senior developer spends a large part of the week on the code other people write: reading their pull requests, answering their questions, and helping the team choose between tools and designs. None of this is a soft extra. A team where reviews are slow and harsh, juniors are stuck and decisions are forgotten ships slowly, whatever the skill of each person. This chapter turns these human tasks into habits as concrete as the code ones.

Bit

The first time someone left forty comments on my pull request, I thought they disliked me. Later I understood that the comments were about the code, and that most of them a script could have written. This chapter is the one I wish I had read then.

18.1 What review is for

A code review is another developer reading a change before it joins the main branch. It has four goals, in this order:

  1. Catch problems early, when they are cheapest to fix: bugs, security holes, missing cases.
  2. Share knowledge. After a review, two people know how the discount code works, not one. Juniors learn by being reviewed, and also by reviewing.
  3. Keep the code consistent, so the codebase reads as if one careful person wrote it.
  4. Keep the design healthy: a change can work today and still make the next ten changes harder.

Notice what is not on the list: proving that the reviewer is clever, or making the code look exactly as the reviewer would have written it. A review that blocks a change for weeks over taste costs more than the problems it finds.

18.2 A pull request that is easy to review

The best review starts before anyone reviews: with a change that is easy to read. Here is Sara, adding discount codes to Bit’s shop. While she was there, her editor also reformatted a stylesheet. Git shows what the pull request contains:

Terminal
# A new branch for the feature
$ git switch -c feature/discount-codes
Switched to a new branch 'feature/discount-codes'
# What changed, file by file
$ git diff --stat main
 src/cart/discount.ts |  11 ++
 src/ui/styles.css    | 300 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++-----------
 2 files changed, 251 insertions(+), 60 deletions(-)

Eleven lines of new feature are hidden among three hundred lines of formatting. A reviewer will either read everything and get tired before reaching the part that matters, or skim and miss it. Both are bad. The fix takes a minute: move the formatting into a pull request of its own, which can be approved at a glance because it changes no behaviour:

Terminal
# Move the formatting into its own pull request
$ git switch -c chore/format-styles main
Switched to a new branch 'chore/format-styles'
$ git checkout feature/discount-codes -- src/ui/styles.css
$ git commit -qm "Format the styles"
$ git switch feature/discount-codes
Switched to branch 'feature/discount-codes'
$ git checkout main -- src/ui/styles.css
$ git commit -qm "Keep only the discount codes"
# The feature’s pull request, after the split
$ git diff --stat main
 src/cart/discount.ts | 11 +++++++++++
 1 file changed, 11 insertions(+)

Now the feature is eleven lines, and the reviewer’s full attention goes to them. Three habits keep pull requests like this:

  • One purpose per pull request. A feature, a fix, a refactoring and a formatting change are four pull requests. Mixed changes are hard to review and hard to undo.
  • Small. Under about 300 changed lines, a reviewer can still hold the whole change in their head. A big feature can be merged in small steps behind a feature flag, a setting that keeps the unfinished part hidden from users.
  • Described. Say what the change does, why, and how to test it. A template in the repository asks for this every time:
.github/pull_request_template.md
## What and why

<!-- One or two sentences. Link the issue: Closes #123 -->

## How to test it

1.
2.

## Screenshots

<!-- Before and after, for any change people can see. Light and dark, LTR and RTL. -->

## Checklist

- [ ] Tests added or changed, and they fail without this change
- [ ] Works with the keyboard and a screen reader
- [ ] Texts in both languages
- [ ] No secrets, no console.log
- [ ] Under 300 changed lines, or the reason it cannot be

GitHub and GitLab put the contents of this file into every new pull request automatically. The checklist is not bureaucracy: each line is a mistake the team has already made once.

18.3 Let the machines go first

A person’s attention is the most expensive thing in a review. It should not be spent on a missing semicolon. Everything a machine can check runs in CI on every pull request before a person is asked to look: the formatter, the linter, the type checker and the tests from Chapter 12. A team can add its own rules too. Here is a small robot reviewer that reads a pull request’s diff:

review-bot.ts
import { readFileSync } from "node:fs";

// Reads the pull request's diff and comments on the lines a machine can judge
type Finding = { file: string; line: number; message: string };

const diff = readFileSync(process.argv[2] ?? "pr.diff", "utf8").split("\n");
const findings: Finding[] = [];
const changedFiles = new Set<string>();
let file = "";
let line = 0;
let changed = 0;

for (const text of diff) {
  if (text.startsWith("+++ ")) {
    file = text.slice(6); // "+++ b/src/cart/discount.ts"
    changedFiles.add(file);
    continue;
  }
  const hunk = /^@@ -\d+(?:,\d+)? \+(\d+)/.exec(text);
  if (hunk) {
    line = Number(hunk[1]); // Line numbers in the new file start from the @@ header of each part of the diff
    continue;
  }
  if (text.startsWith("-") && !text.startsWith("---")) {
    changed++;
    continue;
  }
  if (!text.startsWith("+") || text.startsWith("+++")) {
    if (text.startsWith(" ")) line++;
    continue;
  }
  changed++;
  const code = text.slice(1);
  const say = (message: string) => findings.push({ file, line, message });
  if (/console\.log\(/.test(code)) say("console.log left in: remove it, or use the app's logger");
  if (/:\s*any\b/.test(code)) say("“any” switches the type checker off here: write the real type");
  if (/[^=!]==[^=]/.test(code)) say("“==” converts types before comparing: use “===”");
  if (/TODO(?!.*#\d+)/.test(code)) say("TODO without an issue number: link one (#123), or do it now");
  line++;
}

const isTest = (f: string) => /\.test\.ts$/.test(f);
const code = [...changedFiles].filter((f) => f.endsWith(".ts") && !isTest(f));
if (code.length > 0 && ![...changedFiles].some(isTest)) findings.push({ file: code[0]!, line: 1, message: "code changed, but no test changed" });
if (changed > 300) findings.push({ file: "*", line: 0, message: "{n} changed lines: hard to review well; split it (under 300)".replace("{n}", String(changed)) });

for (const f of findings) console.log(`${f.file}:${f.line}  ${f.message}`);
console.log(findings.length ? `${findings.length} findings` : "Nothing for the robot to say. Over to the humans.");

A diff marks added lines with + and removed lines with -, and each part starts with a header like @@ -0,0 +1,11 @@, which says where the part begins in the new file. The robot follows the line numbers and applies its rules to the added lines only. On Sara’s first pull request:

Terminal
src/cart/discount.ts:3  “any” switches the type checker off here: write the real type
src/cart/discount.ts:4  console.log left in: remove it, or use the app's logger
src/cart/discount.ts:6  “==” converts types before comparing: use “===”
src/cart/discount.ts:7  “==” converts types before comparing: use “===”
src/cart/discount.ts:8  TODO without an issue number: link one (#123), or do it now
src/cart/discount.ts:1  code changed, but no test changed
*:0  311 changed lines: hard to review well; split it (under 300)
7 findings

Seven comments that no person had to write, each pointing at an exact line. Real teams get the same effect with ESLint rules (no-console, eqeqeq, @typescript-eslint/no-explicit-any) and size checks in CI, and they make the checks required, so a pull request cannot be merged while one fails. The rule of thumb: if you write the same review comment three times, automate it.

Quick check

  1. Why should a formatting change never share a pull request with a feature?
  2. What does @@ -0,0 +1,11 @@ tell the robot?
  3. What should you do with a review comment you have written three times?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

18.4 Reviewing like a senior

Now a person reads what is left. Here is the feature part of Sara’s change:

src/cart/discount.ts
diff --git a/src/cart/discount.ts b/src/cart/discount.ts
new file mode 100644
index 0000000..36b91e1
--- /dev/null
+++ b/src/cart/discount.ts
@@ -0,0 +1,11 @@
+import { subtotal } from "./cart";
+
+export function applyDiscount(cart: any, code: string) {
+  console.log("applying", code);
+  let total = subtotal(cart.lines);
+  if (code == "BIT10") total = total * 0.9;
+  if (code == "HALF") total = total / 2;
+  // TODO make the codes configurable
+  cart.total = total;
+  return cart;
+}

The robot already flagged the small things. A useful review goes from the big questions to the small ones, and stops going deeper when a big question fails:

  1. Purpose. Does the change do what the description says, and is it the right thing to build?
  2. Design. Does it fit the code around it? Will the next change be easy?
  3. Correctness. Edge cases, errors, security, money, time zones, languages.
  4. Tests. Would the tests fail if the code were wrong?
  5. Readability. Names, structure, comments where the why is not obvious.

Reading this way, a reviewer finds problems no linter can: total * 0.9 turns whole piastres into fractions; an unknown code silently gives the full price, so a customer who typed bit10 pays more than they expected and nobody knows why; adding a code means editing an if chain; and the function changes the cart it was given, which surprises every caller.

Writing the comments

A comment must say three things: what the problem is, why it matters, and how important it is. Many teams start each comment with a label, in a style called Conventional Comments, so the author never has to guess what is required:

LabelMeansExample on Sara’s change
issue (blocking)Must change before merging“total * 0.9 turns 12997 piastres into 11697.3. We keep money in whole piastres; can we compute the discount and round it once?”
issue (blocking)Must change before merging“An unknown code returns the full price with no message. Customers who mistype will think the discount did not work. Could the result say that the code is unknown?”
suggestionBetter, but the author decides“A table of codes instead of the if chain would let marketing add codes without touching the logic.”
questionThe reviewer wants to understand“Should codes ignore case and spaces? People often paste them with a space at the end.”
nitSmall, never blocking“applyDiscount returns the price, so price might say it better.”
praiseSomething done well“Reusing subtotal instead of a second loop: nice.”

Notice how the comments are written. They are about the code, never the person (“this returns”, not “you forgot”). They explain the consequence for users, which is what makes a reviewer convincing. They ask when the reviewer is not sure. And there is praise: reviews that only ever list problems teach people to dread them. Compare:

Instead ofWrite
“This is wrong.”“This gives a fraction of a piastre for some carts. Could we round the discount once?”
“Why didn’t you use a table?”“suggestion: a table of codes would make adding one a one-line change.”
“Use ===.”(nothing: the linter says it)
Thirty comments on one pull requestThe three that matter, and a call to talk about the design

Tip: review fast

A pull request waiting two days blocks its author, who starts something else and has to switch back later. Many teams agree to give a first review within one working day. A quick review of the important points beats a perfect review next week.

The change after the review

Sara answers every comment and pushes a new version:

src/cart/discount.ts
import { subtotal, type Line } from "./cart";

// Prices are whole piastres: no floating-point money
// The codes live in one table; adding a code needs no new if
const codes: Record<string, { percent: number }> = {
  BIT10: { percent: 10 },
  HALF: { percent: 50 },
};

// Unknown codes are an answer, not a silent full price
export type Priced =
  | { ok: true; subtotal: number; discount: number; total: number }
  | { ok: false; reason: "unknown-code" };

// The cart is not changed: the function returns a new result
export function price(lines: readonly Line[], code?: string): Priced {
  const sum = subtotal(lines);
  const key = code?.trim().toUpperCase();
  if (!key) return { ok: true, subtotal: sum, discount: 0, total: sum };
  const found = codes[key];
  if (!found) return { ok: false, reason: "unknown-code" };
  const discount = Math.round((sum * found.percent) / 100);
  return { ok: true, subtotal: sum, discount, total: sum - discount };
}

And a test that would have caught each of the problems, including the one about changing the cart:

src/cart/discount.test.ts
import { strict as assert } from "node:assert";
import { price } from "./discount";

const cart = [
  { name: "mug", price: 8500, qty: 1 },
  { name: "sticker", price: 1499, qty: 3 },
];
const egp = (piastres: number) => (piastres / 100).toFixed(2);

for (const code of [undefined, "BIT10", " half ", "NOPE"]) {
  const result = price(cart, code);
  const label = (code === undefined ? "(no code)" : JSON.stringify(code)).padEnd(10);
  if (!result.ok) console.log(`${label} → unknown code`);
  else console.log(`${label} → subtotal ${egp(result.subtotal)}  discount ${egp(result.discount)}  total ${egp(result.total)}`);
}

// The cases that matter, as checks that fail loudly
assert.deepEqual(price([], "BIT10"), { ok: true, subtotal: 0, discount: 0, total: 0 });
assert.equal(price(cart, "bit10").ok, true);
assert.deepEqual(price(cart, "FREE"), { ok: false, reason: "unknown-code" });
const before = structuredClone(cart);
price(cart, "HALF");
assert.deepEqual(cart, before);
console.log("✓ 4 checks passed");
Terminal
(no code)  → subtotal 129.97  discount 0.00  total 129.97
"BIT10"    → subtotal 129.97  discount 13.00  total 116.97
" half "   → subtotal 129.97  discount 64.99  total 64.98
"NOPE"     → unknown code
✓ 4 checks passed

Every blocking comment is now a line in the code and a check in the test. That is the best ending a review can have: the problem cannot come back without a test failing.

18.5 Being reviewed

Half of review skill is on the other side. A few habits make it easier for everyone:

  • Review your own pull request first. Read the diff on GitHub before asking anyone. You will find the leftover console.log yourself.
  • Answer every comment, even with “Done in 3f2a91c” or “Good point, I opened #148 for it”. The reviewer should never have to check whether a comment was seen.
  • Disagree with reasons, politely, and in writing. If two rounds of comments do not settle it, talk for five minutes instead.
  • Separate yourself from your code. A comment on a line is not a comment on you. Every senior developer has received thousands.

18.6 Mentoring

Mentoring is helping a less experienced developer grow faster than they would alone. It is part of every senior role, and it is learned like any other skill:

  • Ask before you tell. “What do you think happens when the code is unknown?” teaches more than the answer, because the junior finds it. Give the answer when they are stuck, not before.
  • Pair. In pair programming two people work on one task: the driver types, the navigator thinks ahead. Let the junior drive. Switch every half hour or so.
  • Give tasks slightly too big. A task they can do with some help, not one they can already do, nor one that will drown them. Agree in advance when they should ask for help: for example, after an hour stuck.
  • Give feedback on behaviour, soon and specifically. A simple shape is situation, behaviour, impact: “In yesterday’s pull request (situation), you added tests for the unknown code (behaviour); it stopped a bug that would have reached customers (impact).” It works for praise and for problems, and it never says “you always”.
  • Make it safe to not know. Say “I don’t know, let’s find out” yourself. Juniors who are afraid to ask lose days in silence.

A first week plan for a new team member, built on these habits:

DayGoal
1The app runs on their computer; they know who to ask about what
2A tiny real change (a text, a colour) goes through the whole flow: branch, pull request, review, merge, release
3–4A small bug, paired with you for the first hour
5They review someone else’s pull request, and you review their review

18.7 Technical decisions

Teams decide things all the time: which library, which structure, whether to rewrite. A senior’s job is not to decide alone, but to make sure decisions are made deliberately, by the right people, and remembered.

Two kinds of doors

First ask how hard the decision is to undo. A two-way door, such as a helper library used in one screen, can be reversed in an afternoon: decide quickly, and let the person doing the work choose. A one-way door, such as the main framework, the database, or the shape of a public API, is expensive to reverse: slow down, compare options, and write it down. Most decisions are two-way doors, and treating them like one-way doors is how teams get stuck in meetings.

A decision matrix

For a real choice, list the criteria first, give each a weight for how much it matters to this team, and only then score the options. Choosing the criteria before looking at the options keeps everyone honest. Here is the team choosing a tool for end-to-end tests:

decide.ts
// Every score is from 1 (poor) to 5 (excellent); the weights say what matters to this team
const criteria = { fit: 5, learn: 3, speed: 3, community: 2, cost: 2 } as const;
type Criterion = keyof typeof criteria;

const names: Record<Criterion, string> = {
  fit: "Fits our stack", learn: "Easy to learn", speed: "Speed of the tests", community: "Community and docs", cost: "Cost",
};

const options: Record<string, Record<Criterion, number>> = {
  Playwright: { fit: 5, learn: 3, speed: 4, community: 4, cost: 5 },
  Cypress: { fit: 4, learn: 5, speed: 3, community: 5, cost: 4 },
  "Manual testing only": { fit: 2, learn: 5, speed: 1, community: 3, cost: 2 },
};

function total(scores: Record<Criterion, number>, weights: Record<Criterion, number>): number {
  return (Object.keys(weights) as Criterion[]).reduce((sum, c) => sum + scores[c] * weights[c], 0);
}

function winner(weights: Record<Criterion, number>): string {
  return Object.entries(options).sort(([, a], [, b]) => total(b, weights) - total(a, weights))[0]![0];
}

console.log("Option".padEnd(22) + "score");
for (const [name, scores] of Object.entries(options)) console.log(name.padEnd(22) + total(scores, criteria));
console.log(`Winner: ${winner(criteria)}`);

// Which option would win if one criterion mattered twice as much?
for (const c of Object.keys(criteria) as Criterion[]) {
  console.log(`  If "${names[c]}" counted twice → ${winner({ ...criteria, [c]: criteria[c] * 2 })}`);
}
Terminal
Option                score
Playwright            64
Cypress               62
Manual testing only   38
Winner: Playwright
  If "Fits our stack" counted twice → Playwright
  If "Easy to learn" counted twice → Cypress
  If "Speed of the tests" counted twice → Playwright
  If "Community and docs" counted twice → Playwright
  If "Cost" counted twice → Playwright

Playwright wins, but only by two points, and the last lines show something more useful than the winner: if learning speed mattered twice as much, Cypress would win. So the real question for the team is “how much does a quick start matter to us?”. A matrix does not make the decision; it shows which disagreement is worth having.

When the scores are guesses, buy information with a spike: a short, time-boxed experiment, such as a day to write the same three tests with each tool. The spike’s code is thrown away; its lessons go into the scores.

Decision records

Six months later, someone new will ask “why Playwright?”. If the answer lives only in a meeting, it is lost. An architecture decision record (ADR) is a short file, kept in the repository next to the code, that records one decision: its context, the options, the choice and its consequences:

docs/decisions/0007-e2e-tests.md
# 7. Use Playwright for end-to-end tests

Date: 2026-03-04
Status: Accepted

## Context

Bugs reached users twice last month through the checkout, which no test covers
end to end. We need tests that click through the real app in a real browser,
and run on every pull request in under five minutes.

## Options

- Playwright: 64 points in our matrix (decide.ts)
- Cypress: 62 points; easier to learn, and wins if that counts double
- Manual testing before each release: 38 points

## Decision

We use Playwright, because it fits our tools best (TypeScript, several
browsers, runs in parallel) and is free. The vote was close: two people
on the team will pair with the others on the first tests.

## Consequences

- Good: the checkout is tested on every pull request, in three browsers.
- Bad: one more tool to learn; the tests need a running app in CI.
- We will look again if the suite takes more than five minutes.

Records are numbered and never edited after they are accepted. When a decision changes, a new record says so and marks the old one “Replaced by 12”. The folder becomes the history of why the code is the way it is, and it is reviewed in pull requests like any code.

18.8 Technical debt

Technical debt is the extra work that every future change costs because of a shortcut taken earlier. Like money, debt is not always bad: shipping a feature by Friday with a known shortcut can be the right decision, if it is a decision. The trouble is debt nobody chose or recorded.

  • Make it visible. Every shortcut gets an issue, and the TODO in the code links to it, which is exactly what the robot checks.
  • Pay it continuously. Many teams reserve a fixed share of every sprint, often around a fifth, for debt. Waiting for a “cleanup month” means waiting forever.
  • Pay where you work. Improve the code you are already changing, a little, every time. Debt in code nobody touches costs nothing and can wait.
  • Explain it in the business’s words. Not “the cart code is ugly”, but “every change to prices takes three days instead of one, and broke checkout twice this year”.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Huge pull requestsTired reviewers approve without readingOne purpose, under about 300 lines; feature flags for big work
People checking style by handSlow reviews full of small commentsFormatter, linter and robot rules in CI, required to pass
Comments without a levelAuthors treat every nit as an orderLabels: issue, suggestion, question, nit, praise
Comments about the personDefensive authors, worse codeWrite about the code and its effect on users
Reviews that wait for daysBlocked authors and costly context switchesA first review within a working day
Giving juniors the answersThey stay dependent on youAsk questions first; pair with them driving
Decisions made in meetings and forgottenThe same debate every six monthsA decision record in the repository
Unrecorded shortcutsDebt that grows unseenAn issue for every shortcut, a share of every sprint to pay it

Summary

  • Review catches problems, shares knowledge, keeps code consistent and the design healthy. It is not about taste.
  • Pull requests have one purpose, stay small, and are described with a template.
  • Machines go first: formatting, linting, types, tests and team rules run in CI. Automate any comment written three times.
  • Review from purpose and design down to correctness, tests and readability. Label each comment, write about the code, explain the impact, and praise what is good.
  • When reviewed: review yourself first, answer every comment, and disagree with reasons.
  • Mentor by asking before telling, pairing, stretching tasks and specific feedback.
  • Decide fast on two-way doors; for one-way doors use a weighted matrix, spikes, and an ADR.
  • Technical debt is fine when chosen and recorded; pay it continuously where you work.

Quick check answers: 1. Because the formatting hides the feature: the reviewer either tires before reaching it or skims past it, and the two cannot be undone separately. 2. That the next lines are the start of the new file (line 1, eleven lines), so the robot can number each added line. 3. Automate it: a lint rule, a CI check, or a line in the robot.

Practice projects

These projects are about habits more than code. Do each one with another person if you can: a classmate, a colleague, or a friend from an online community. Review and mentoring are learned with someone on the other side.

Project 18.1 · Easy

Review your own old code

Take a project you wrote a few months ago, open a pull request that improves one thing, and review it as if a colleague had written it.

You will practise: small focused pull requests, the review order, labelled comments, and answering every comment.

  1. Choose one improvement only: a function without tests, a confusing name, a missing error state. Make it on a branch, under 100 changed lines.
  2. Fill in the pull request template from this chapter, including how to test it.
  3. Wait a day. Then review the pull request on GitHub in the order purpose, design, correctness, tests, readability, and leave at least five labelled comments, including one praise.
  4. Answer every comment as the author: fix it, open an issue for it, or disagree with a reason.

Hint: reviewing your own code after a day is surprisingly effective, because you have forgotten what you meant and can only read what you wrote. That is exactly the position of every reviewer.

Project 18.2 · Medium

A robot reviewer in CI

Run the chapter’s review robot on every pull request in GitHub Actions, and make it fail the check when it finds a problem.

You will practise: reading diffs, exit codes, GitHub Actions workflows, and required checks.

  1. Copy review-bot.ts into a repository of yours, and make it end with process.exitCode = findings.length ? 1 : 0.
  2. Write .github/workflows/review.yml: on pull_request, check out the code with fetch-depth: 0, run git diff origin/${{ github.base_ref }}...HEAD > pr.diff, then run the robot.
  3. Open a pull request with a console.log in it and check that the robot fails. Remove it and check that it passes.
  4. In the repository settings, make the check required for merging into the main branch.
  5. Add one rule that your own team (or you) keeps forgetting.

Hint: the three dots in main...HEAD matter. They compare against the point where your branch started, so changes merged into main since then are not counted as yours.

Project 18.3 · Challenge

A decision you can defend

Choose between two real libraries for your project with a time-boxed spike and a weighted matrix, write the decision record, and present it in ten minutes.

You will practise: one-way doors, criteria and weights, spikes, sensitivity, and decision records.

  1. Pick a real choice, for example dates (date-fns or the built-in Intl and Temporal), or charts (two chart libraries). Decide whether it is a one-way or two-way door.
  2. Write the criteria and their weights before you try anything. Include bundle size, measured as in Chapter 13.
  3. Spend at most half a day per option building the same small screen. Write down surprises as you go.
  4. Score the options in a copy of decide.ts and run the sensitivity check. Find the criterion that would change the winner.
  5. Write the decision record, including the bad consequences of your choice, and present it to someone in ten minutes. Note their questions: each one is something the record should have answered.

Hint: if no weight change can flip the winner, the decision was easy and the matrix was not needed. If many can, the scores are probably guesses: that is the moment for a spike, not a longer meeting.

Exercises

  1. Easy Rewrite these comments using a label and the impact: “Bad name.”, “Why is this here??”, “You forgot the tests again.”
  2. Easy Add a rule to review-bot.ts that flags debugger statements, and run it on pr.diff after adding a debugger line to it.
  3. Medium The robot counts a file as a test only if it ends in .test.ts. Change it so that a pull request touching src/cart/discount.ts needs a change to src/cart/discount.test.ts in particular, not to any test file.
  4. Medium In decide.ts, add a criterion “Debugging tools” with weight 3 (Playwright 5, Cypress 5, manual 1). Does the winner change? Does the sensitivity check change?
  5. Challenge Write a decision record for a decision your own project made without one (a framework, a CSS approach, a hosting provider). Include at least two options you rejected and one bad consequence of the choice.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 19

Everything in this book comes together in one last project: a production dashboard, built, tested, reviewed and shipped the way a professional team would do it.

Appendix

Answers to the Exercises

Try every exercise yourself before you look here. When your answer differs from this one, that is not necessarily wrong: most problems have several good solutions. If yours handles normal and unusual cases, and you can explain your choices to a colleague, it is a good answer. The full, tested code for every answer is in the book’s code repository.

Chapter 1

  1. const removeItem = (cart, id) => ({ ...cart, items: cart.items.filter((item) => item.id !== id) }); With two items, the original still has 2, the result has 1, and cart === next is false.
  2. Object.groupBy(words, (word) => word[0]) gives { a: ["apple", "avocado"], b: ["banana", "blueberry"], c: ["cherry"] }. Node prints it as [Object: null prototype]: the result has no prototype, so a key such as "constructor" can never clash with a built-in.
  3. Define class InsufficientFundsError extends Error and set this.name in its constructor. In withdraw, throw it when amount > this.#balance; otherwise subtract and return this. Test a withdrawal that works (100 − 30 leaves 70) and one that throws, checking error instanceof InsufficientFundsError.
  4. C, B, A: synchronous code first, then the microtask from the promise, then the timer task.
  5. function* take(iterable, n) { if (n <= 0) return; let count = 0; for (const value of iterable) { yield value; if (++count >= n) return; } }. With function* evens() { for (let n = 0; ; n += 2) yield n; }, [...take(evens(), 5)] is [0, 2, 4, 6, 8]. Returning from the generator stops the loop, so the endless generator is never asked for a sixth value.

Chapter 2

  1. type Book = { readonly isbn: string; title: string; subtitle?: string; authors: string[] }, and label returns book.subtitle ? `${book.title}: ${book.subtitle}` : book.title. TypeScript forces the check, because subtitle may be undefined.
  2. const SIZES = ["S", "M", "L"] as const; type Size = (typeof SIZES)[number]; and SIZES.length is 3. Adding "XL" to the array updates the type by itself.
  3. The check reports that { status: "empty" } is not assignable to never in the default branch. Add case "empty": return "Nothing here yet"; (or any message you like) and the error disappears.
  4. last can simply return list.at(-1). For groupBy, start with const groups = {} as Record<K, T[]>, then for each item write (groups[key(item)] ??= []).push(item). This is one of the few honest uses of as: the object starts empty and is filled by the loop.
  5. Write isProduct(value: unknown): value is Product that checks the object and each field with typeof, then isProductList = (value: unknown): value is Product[] => Array.isArray(value) && value.every(isProduct). Good data passes; the price written as a string and the object that is not an array are both rejected.

Chapter 3

  1. In prices.mjs add export const FREE_SHIPPING = 50;; in index.mjs change the second line to export { default as formatPrice, VAT, FREE_SHIPPING } from "./prices.mjs";; then import it in main.mjs with the others and print it.
  2. The JavaScript file gets a new name, because its content changed, and so does index.html’s content, because it points to the new name. The CSS file keeps its name: it did not change, so visitors who already have it in their cache do not download it again.
  3. ^1.4.2 allows 1.4.9 and 1.9.0, but not 1.4.1 (older) or 2.0.0 (a new major version). ~1.4.2 allows only 1.4.9 from the list: it accepts patch updates within 1.4.
  4. ESLint reports 'x' is never reassigned. Use 'const' instead. Change let to const, or run npx eslint src --fix, which makes this change by itself.
  5. Add a second button whose handler runs const { showHelp } = await import("./help.ts"). The build then lists report-….js and help-….js next to index-….js: one extra file per dynamic import.

Chapter 4

  1. Price.vue: defineProps<{ value: number; currency: string }>() and the template <p class="price">{{ value.toFixed(2) }} {{ currency }}</p>. In ProductCard: <Price :value="product.price" currency="EGP" />. Notice the colon on :value but not on currency, which is a fixed string.
  2. Add discount?: number to Product, and in the card <span v-if="product.discount !== undefined" class="badge">-{{ product.discount * 100 }}%</span>.
  3. In Shop.vue: const count = ref(0); on each card @add="count++"; and above the grid <p>{{ count }} items in your cart</p>. The state lives in Shop because the cards only report the click; the shop owns the cart.
  4. Add | { status: "offline" } to the type, and a branch <p v-else-if="state.status === 'offline'" class="muted">You are offline.</p> before the last one. vue-tsc helps: until you add the branch, state.orders in the last branch is an error, because state might be offline there. It cannot tell you that the new state needs a nice message; that is still your job.
  5. In ShopSection.vue: <header><h2>{{ title }}</h2><slot name="actions" /></header>. An empty named slot draws nothing, so the slot is optional already. In Shop.vue: <template #actions><a href="/products">See all</a></template>. To avoid an empty wrapper when it is missing, use v-if="$slots.actions".

Chapter 5

  1. <button type="button" @click="lines = []">Clear</button>. None of the computed values change: the count, the total and the missing amount are all calculated from lines, so they follow it by themselves. That is the reward for minimal state.
  2. One more line, Total: 24: removing the book leaves three mugs at 8. pop() changes the array, the array was read by total, so the effect runs again.
  3. Reset: @click="seconds = 0". The title: watchEffect(() => { document.title = running.value ? `${seconds.value} s` : "Timer"; }). Stopped, it should go back to the page’s normal title, so the tab does not keep showing an old number.
  4. In QuantityInput.vue: const note = defineModel<string>("note", { default: "" }) and an input with v-model="note". In the parent: <QuantityInput v-model="mugs" v-model:note="giftNote" … />.
  5. watch(lines, (now) => localStorage.setItem("cart", JSON.stringify(now)), { deep: true }), and start with ref<Line[]>(load()). Old or broken data would crash the screen, so load parses inside try, checks the shape (for example with a zod schema, Chapter 2), and falls back to []. Saving a version number with the data lets a later release convert old carts instead of throwing them away.

Chapter 6

  1. const laps = ref<number[]>([]); const lap = () => laps.value.push(seconds.value);, cleared in reset, and returned as laps: readonly(laps). A caller who tries laps.value.push(9) gets a warning, and TypeScript refuses it.
  2. export function useToggle(initial: Ref<boolean> | boolean = false) { const value = isRef(initial) ? initial : ref(initial); return { value, toggle: () => (value.value = !value.value) }; }. Accepting a ref matters for the theme: const dark = useLocalStorage("dark", false); const { toggle } = useToggle(dark); toggles the saved ref itself. Passing dark.value instead would copy the boolean, and the choice would no longer be saved.
  3. const list = matchMedia(query); const matches = ref(list.matches); const update = (e: MediaQueryListEvent) => (matches.value = e.matches); list.addEventListener("change", update); onScopeDispose(() => list.removeEventListener("change", update)); return matches;. In the template: {{ wide ? "wide" : "narrow" }}.
  4. Inside effectScope and with fake timers: set the query to “b”, await nextTick(), advance 100 ms, set it to “bit”, await nextTick(), then advance 500 ms and await the promises (await vi.runAllTimersAsync()). Expect results.value to hold only names containing “bit”. The test fails if you remove onCleanup(() => controller.abort()) and make the first search slower.
  5. useAsync<I, O>(fn: (input: I, signal: AbortSignal) => Promise<O>, input: MaybeRefOrGetter<I>) holds the watcher, the controller, data, loading and error. Then useSearch = (q) => useAsync((text, signal) => (text ? searchProducts(text, signal) : Promise.resolve([])), () => toValue(q).trim()). This is the idea that TanStack Query, in the next chapter, takes much further.

Chapter 7

  1. Add { path: "about", component: About } to the children of the layout route, and <RouterLink to="/about">About</RouterLink> to the navigation bar. The active class is added by itself.
  2. const client = useQueryClient(); const position = computed(() => { const list = client.getQueryData<Product[]>(["products", null]); const i = list?.findIndex((p) => p.id === props.id) ?? -1; return list && i >= 0 ? `Product ${i + 1} of ${list.length}` : ""; });. Reading the cache makes no request.
  3. const q = computed(() => (typeof route.query.q === "string" ? route.query.q : "")), add q to the key, and filter with p.name.toLowerCase().includes(q.value.toLowerCase()). Update it as in Project 7.2, with router.replace and the other parameters spread.
  4. Add meta: { title: "Products" } to each route, and router.afterEach((to) => { document.title = `${to.meta.title ?? "Bit's shop"} · Bit's shop`; }). To make to.meta.title typed, extend the RouteMeta interface in a .d.ts file.
  5. const client = useQueryClient(); watchEffect(() => client.prefetchQuery({ queryKey: ["product", props.id + 1], queryFn: () => getProduct(props.id + 1) })); and <RouterLink :to="`/products/${id + 1}`">. When the user clicks, the query finds the product in the cache and shows it without a skeleton.

Chapter 8

  1. In the schema: phone: z.union([z.literal(""), z.string().regex(/^\d{11}$/, "Please enter 11 digits.")]), with phone: "" in the initial values and a FormField like the others. The empty string is allowed explicitly; anything else must be 11 digits.
  2. <p class="muted">{{ values.message.trim().length }} of at least 20 characters</p>. Because values is reactive, the counter follows every keystroke without any extra code.
  3. Keep a copy of initial, and const dirty = computed(() => JSON.stringify(values) !== JSON.stringify(initial)). In the component: useEventListener(window, "beforeunload", (e) => { if (dirty.value) e.preventDefault(); }). The browser then asks the user before leaving.
  4. Start with useForm(contactSchema, loadDraft() ?? empty), where loadDraft parses localStorage inside try, and save with watch(values, (v) => localStorage.setItem("contact-draft", JSON.stringify(v)), { deep: true }). Remove the draft after a successful send.
  5. Watch the email together with a “touched” flag: when the field has been left and the email is valid, debounce it (Chapter 6), call a pretend isTaken(email, signal), cancel stale calls in the cleanup, and put the answer in a separate emailTaken ref shown under the field. The submit function still asks the server again, because the answer may have changed.

Chapter 9

  1. In the store: function remove(id: number) { delete lines.value[id]; }, returned with the others. Deleting a property of a reactive object is seen by Vue, so the badge and total update. In CartSummary: <button type="button" @click="cart.remove(l.product.id)">×</button>.
  2. export const useLanguage = defineStore("language", () => { const lang = ref<"en" | "ar">("en"); watchEffect(() => { document.documentElement.lang = lang.value; document.documentElement.dir = lang.value === "ar" ? "rtl" : "ltr"; }); return { lang }; });. The watcher lives as long as the store, which is the whole life of the app.
  3. setActivePinia(createPinia()); const cart = useCart(); cart.add(book); cart.add(mug); cart.add(mug); cart.clear();, printing cart.count after each: 1, 2, 3, 0. In Node there is no window, so useLocalStorage quietly behaves like a plain ref: the store works, and nothing is saved. That makes the store easy to test.
  4. if (import.meta.env.DEV) cart.$subscribe(() => console.log(new Date().toISOString(), JSON.stringify(cart.lines)));, in main.ts after installing Pinia. The build removes the whole branch in production, because import.meta.env.DEV becomes false.
  5. type Auth = { status: "signed-out" } | { status: "signing-in" } | { status: "signed-in"; user: User } in a ref; signIn sets signing-in, awaits the pretend API, then sets signed-in. The template uses v-if on state.status. As a Pinia store the code is almost the same, minus the key and the two composables, and it appears in the devtools; as provide it can be scoped to a part of the app, for example a demo area with a fake user.

Chapter 10

  1. Add "8": "32px" to space and "lg": "16px" to radius, run the script, and write border-radius: var(--radius-lg) in the card style. The script prints 6 spaces and 4 radii.
  2. For example "focus": "pink-60" in the dark theme. The script stops with Unknown primitive token: pink-60, naming the exact value to fix. Without the check, the dark theme would silently have no focus colour.
  3. Add a "contrast" object under semantic with every semantic name, and generate a third block for [data-theme="contrast"]. No component changes: they only use semantic tokens. If one did need a change, it was using a hard-coded value, and that is the real bug.
  4. Add a named slot, <slot name="iconStart" />, wrapped in a span with aria-hidden="true" before the default slot. The gap on .btn already spaces it correctly in both directions, because flexbox follows the text direction.
  5. Add "duration": { "fast": "150ms", "normal": "250ms" } to the tokens, generate --duration-fast and --duration-normal, and use transition: inset-inline-start var(--duration-normal) in the switch. Then have the script also write @media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) { :root { --duration-fast: 0ms; --duration-normal: 0ms; } }. Every component using the tokens stops moving, without a single change.

Chapter 11

  1. Add aria-label to the icon button (with aria-hidden on the icon), an alt to the image, and a <label> to the email field. The audit then still reports the contrast, the missing lang and the three structure rules: “critical” is not the same as “all”.
  2. Typical findings: focus that disappears behind a sticky header or a cookie banner; a size picker made of divs that Tab skips; a pop-up that opens without moving the focus; a “Pay” button that cannot be reached until a hidden checkbox is ticked with the mouse.
  3. In package.json: "check": "tsc && eslint src && prettier --check src && vite build && node scripts/audit-all.mjs". The audit runs on the built pages, which are what users receive.
  4. Add the RouteFocus component from Project 11.3 to the layout of the routing chapter’s app, inside the router. Each page already has one h1 inside main, which is all the component needs.
  5. Common discoveries: images described by their file names; icon buttons read as “button”; the same “Read more” link ten times; errors that appear but are never read; and pages that change silently. Each one is a small fix, and each one now has a name you know from this chapter.

Chapter 12

  1. it("treats a quantity of 0 as no item", () => { expect(cartTotal([{ price: 25, quantity: 0 }])).toBe(0); });. Zero is allowed (only negative quantities throw), and adds nothing. Deciding the answer first is the point: the test records a decision.
  2. If the coupon is ignored, the two it.each rows fail. Vitest shows each failing test’s name, the line of the assertion, and expected 41 to be 36.9: the name says which rule broke, and the values say how.
